# deepseek-harness / dsh-notes-arch-feature#1

## Shard shape

This shard is the entire archived **feature** design-note corpus of the deepseek-harness repo: 260 files under `.agents/notes/archived/feature/`, each dated note published in three parts — an English `.md` decision note, a line-for-line Chinese `.zh.md` translation of it, and a machine-written `.i18n.yaml` bilingual-pairing record holding the two sides' git blob hashes. The notes run 2026-06-14 → 2026-07-26 and they are the *architecture* layer of the harness: they record the decisions behind the ACP editor bridge and its multi-session multiplexing, the filesystem tool schemas, the compaction capability seam, the subagent capability seam plus its ACP and in-process backends, workspace-instruction loading, the ask-user-question and todo tools, the hook bridges and interception points, the harness-level agent loop, the sandbox, the MCP client, plan mode, session prefixing, the repeat-tool guard, the session-query service and its model-facing tools, provider retry policies, the PTC / Code-Mode dispatch stack, and roughly thirty TUI/Web surface notes. Every other shard of this repo reads *code*; this one reads *why the code has the shape it has*, which makes it the densest available source of transferable mechanism — the "Alternatives considered" sections in particular name what was rejected and why. The notes are archived because each decision was later folded into a later note or superseded, but the reasoning is intact and the file names are stable, so a mechanism can be traced from its decision to its successor.

## Per-file notes

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-14-acp-agent-client-protocol.md
- Purpose: Records the retired editor-facing ACP (Agent Client Protocol) bridge — the JSON-RPC-over-stdio surface that let an external editor (Zed) create/resume/prompt/cancel harness sessions.
- Key abstractions: `dsh-acp` plugin as a UI/client-driver only (no agent-loop dependency); `initialize` / `session/new` / `session/load` / `session/prompt` / `session/cancel`; tool "render intent" union `generic|terminal|diff`; permission presets as a single `permission` config-option select; `AgentHandle` per live session.
- Data flow: `initialize` negotiates version + advertises text/`resource_link` prompts and `loadSession`; `session/new` validates an absolute `cwd` into `SessionHeader` and creates the agent via `ctx.agents`; `session/prompt` settles against that prompt's owning `turn/end`; tool calls render through the tool's own `presentCall`/`presentResult`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: the bridge programs *interface* services only, so it can never depend on the concrete agent loop, bypass the tool registry, or execute shell in the editor (L14, L44). Permission is an *answerer* on the shared user-approval seam, not an ask-every-tool policy — the asking plugin owns the decision to ask (L30). Bash terminal metadata (`_meta.terminal_info|terminal_output|terminal_exit`) is presentation-only; execution stays in `ctx.bash` so sandbox/env-scrub/cwd survive (L28).
- Edge cases / failure handling: foreign or call-less `approval/request`s are delegated and a missing answerer stays fail-closed; unsupported/empty prompt content is rejected; one in-flight prompt per session; disconnect/dispose cancel pending prompts, dispose handles in parallel, then await loop quiescence and persistence flush before dropping records (L36).
- Surprising: stdout is the protocol transport, so the ACP app composition loads *no* stdout logger and a test guards stdout as framed JSON-RPC only (L36); an idle config selection is truthful in the live response but not durable until the next `agent/prompt-submit` anchors it (L58).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-14-acp-agent-client-protocol.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-06-14-acp-agent-client-protocol.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the retired editor-facing ACP bridge note (same 62 lines, same section order: 问题/决策/曾考虑的替代方案/后果/验证).
- Key abstractions: Same as the English twin — `dsh-acp` plugin, the five ACP session methods, the `generic|terminal|diff` render-intent union, the `permission` config-option select, `AgentHandle`.
- Data flow: Identical protocol path; the translation keeps the "one in-flight prompt per session, settles on its owning `turn/end`" rule and the `ctx.agents` creation route.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Same three rejections as the English side (inject the concrete `agentLoop`; execute bash through ACP `terminal/*`; model permission presets as ACP session modes) with the same reasoning — mechanism / asking-policy / UI-answerer stay separated.
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same fail-closed answerer rule, same disposal quiescence ordering (cancel → parallel dispose → await quiescence + flush → remove records).
- Surprising: Carries the same "no stdout logger" purity rule (L36) and the same durability caveat for idle config selections (L58); it also links the Chinese counterpart of the superseding simplification note (`2026-07-23-acp-automation-only-protocol.zh.md`, L8).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-14-acp-agent-client-protocol.i18n.yaml
- Purpose: Bilingual-pair consistency record — stores the git blob hash of the English and Chinese sides of this note as of the last confirmed-consistent state.
- Key abstractions: two `path: sha1` lines; the header names the repair command `pnpm run verify-translation-pairing --write`.
- Data flow: Read by the translation-pairing verifier to detect a one-sided edit to either `.md`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The file states "Both languages carry equal authority" (L2) — the Chinese side is not a derived artifact, so an edit to one must be carried into the other and re-recorded.
- Edge cases / failure handling: A stale/mismatched hash pair is what the verifier fails on; the note is regenerated rather than hand-edited.
- Surprising: The hashes are the *only* content — 6 lines and nothing else, so 87 of these files exist purely to make a one-sided translation edit mechanically detectable.

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-14-acp-multi-session.md
- Purpose: Decides how several concurrent ACP sessions are multiplexed over one agent subprocess without any session's answer, prompt, cancellation, permission request, or background-job id leaking into another.
- Key abstractions: `Map<SessionId, AcpSession>` forward map; `ownedRecord` exact-agent-object ownership check; per-session one in-flight prompt carrying its own durable turn number; opaque per-session owner token for background bash jobs; memoized per-session close shared by `session/close`, teardown and plugin disposal.
- Data flow: Every `session/event` resolves the owning record *before* sending or settling; a prompt captures its own user-sourced `turn/start` and settles only on the matching `turn/end`; `session/cancel` addresses one record and calls only that agent's queue-aware cancel path.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Ownership is decided by *exact agent object identity*, not by id — a foreign object with the same id cannot claim the session (L16). Background jobs carry an opaque owner token equal to the session id rather than relying on agent object identity, because a resumed/replaced agent object may legitimately be the same durable session and the token must survive plugin reloads (L22, L40). `ctx.sandboxPolicy` resolves each session's `cwd` as its own `workspace-write` root, so shared bash/fs services serve concurrent projects without cross-project writes (L32).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Injection turns, autonomous plugin/goal turns, and a late `turn/end` from a cancelled prior turn cannot settle a prompt (L18); `session/resume` rejects a still-live id so one durable session cannot get two Agent objects; exact records leave the map only after their close settles.
- Surprising: A per-session `ctx.extend()` was rejected because a child context does not create a child plugin fiber — listeners would still belong to the bridge fiber, so the implementation uses global listeners plus explicit O(1) demultiplexing (L38).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-14-acp-multi-session.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-06-14-acp-multi-session.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the multi-session multiplexing decision (same 50 lines and section order).
- Key abstractions: Same `Map<SessionId, AcpSession>` forward map, `ownedRecord` exact-agent check, per-session in-flight prompt with durable turn number, opaque job owner token, memoized close.
- Data flow: Same ordering rule — `session/event` resolves the owning record before any send or settle; `session/cancel` touches exactly one record.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Same three rejections (one live session per connection; per-session `ctx.extend()`; agent-object identity as bash-job ownership) with the same reasoning about plugin fibers and durable-session identity.
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same protections against injection/autonomous/late-end prompt settlement and against double-Agent resume.
- Surprising: Keeps the same non-obvious sandbox point — `ctx.sandboxPolicy` makes each session's `cwd` its own `workspace-write` root so one connection can serve concurrent projects (L32) — and it also documents that the automation bridge advertises no multi-root capability and rejects non-empty `additionalDirectories` (L30).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-17-filesystem-tool-schemas.md
- Purpose: Fixes the model-facing argument shapes of the first filesystem tool suite (`read`, `write`, `edit`) exposed by `@deepseek-ai/dsh-tool-fs`.
- Key abstractions: `read(file_path, offset?, limit?)`, `write(file_path, content)`, `edit(file_path, old_string, new_string, replace_all?)`; the `ctx.fs` capability seam; the `dsh-fs-observation-policy` plugin; the canonical tool-output contract's `output.render` projection.
- Data flow: The consumer package translates model-facing names into `ctx.fs` calls and `fs/*` event dispatches; `ctx.fs` keeps the structured result facts (returned lines, line counts, display path, file version, partial-view flag) and the model text is derived through `output.render` (L74).
- Non-obvious design decisions: snake_case field names, chosen to match Claude Code and existing harness schemas, because "naming becomes public API" once shipped (L24, L112). No model-facing `expected_hash` / `expected_version` / `create_only` — stale checks are driven by backend-minted versions and the policy plugin's observed state, never by a model copying a token (L55, L105). One strict literal-replacement mode instead of a Codex-style patch grammar, so the backend owns exact-match/duplicate/line-ending semantics (L70).
- Edge cases / failure handling: `edit` rejects an empty `old_string`; `replace_all` defaults to false and then `old_string` must match exactly once; `write` to an *existing* file requires a prior observation by the same execution context while creating a *new* file does not; with the policy plugin absent `write` degrades to an unconditional create-or-overwrite (L53).
- Surprising: Under the observation policy, *any windowed read counts* as authorization — freshness of the observed version, not a full-view requirement, is what the gate checks (L68); the structured result deliberately does not restate model arguments unless the backend resolved them into new information (L84).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-17-filesystem-tool-schemas.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-06-17-filesystem-tool-schemas.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the model-facing filesystem schema decision (same 113 lines, same schema table and result-shape table).
- Key abstractions: Same three tools with the same snake_case argument sets, the `ctx.fs` seam, the observation-policy plugin, and the `output.render` model projection.
- Data flow: Same translation duty — model-facing names are translated into `ctx.fs` calls and `fs/*` dispatches by the consumer package, with file-state recording staying on `ctx.fs`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Same three rejections (Codex patch grammar / multi-mode edit API; camelCase argument names; model-facing hash/version/create_only parameters) with the same "naming is public API" reasoning (L104, L111).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same empty-`old_string` rejection, `replace_all` default, prior-observation requirement for updating an existing file, and the "policy plugin absent ⇒ unconditional write" degradation (L53).
- Surprising: Faithfully preserves the "any windowed read counts as an observation" rule and the note that the structured outcome must not restate model arguments (L68, L84).
### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-18-acp-terminal-and-tool-rendering.md
- Purpose: Decides how a `bash` tool call becomes a rich terminal card (cwd, command, output, exit status) in an ACP client without moving command execution out of the harness.
- Key abstractions: the ACP `_meta` extensibility point; the Zed convention keys `_meta.terminal_info` / `_meta.terminal_output` / `_meta.terminal_exit`; `clientCapabilities._meta.terminal_output` as the capability gate; a provider-neutral terminal presentation struct in `dsh-tools` (`cwd`, `data`, `exitCode`/`signal`).
- Data flow: `initialize` records the capability per connection; `dsh-tool-bash` returns the neutral terminal presentation from the resolved workdir and run result; the bridge maps it to `content:[{type:'terminal', terminalId}]` + `_meta.terminal_info` on the call, and `_meta.terminal_output` + `_meta.terminal_exit` on the update; `terminalId` is derived from the harness `callId`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: the ACP *client-side* terminal sub-protocol (`terminal/create`, where the editor executes) was rejected because it would bypass `dsh-bash`'s env scrub, background-task ownership and per-session cwd, forking execution into two backends — both reference agents (`claude-agent-acp`, `codex-acp`) were checked and do the same (L18–25). The `tool_call_update`'s text `content` is deliberately **omitted** when the terminal card is used, because an ACP update's `content` *replaces* the call's content collection and would clobber the terminal block (L33). The exit code is recovered by **parsing the status markers** (`[exit code: N]`) that `renderResult` emitted, because the pure `presentResult(args, result)` seam only sees content blocks — the parse is the exact inverse of the marker emission, they co-evolve in one file, and a round-trip test pins the pair (L34).
- Edge cases / failure handling: a client that did not advertise `_meta.terminal_output` still receives the ` ```console ` fenced text block — the text fallback is the contract for everyone else and a no-capability test asserts it; `terminalId` is derived per-call so it cannot collide; the bridge never creates a client-side terminal, so disposal needs no new teardown.
- Surprising: the `_meta` keys are a *Zed convention, not ACP spec* — `_meta` is spec-blessed but the specific keys are de-facto contract (L25); live incremental streaming (`_meta.terminal_output_delta`) and command classification (rendering `cat` as a read card) were explicitly deferred, with the rule that command classification must be display-only and never change what executes (L51).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-18-acp-terminal-and-tool-rendering.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-06-18-acp-terminal-and-tool-rendering.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the `_meta`-convention terminal-card decision (same 51 lines, same "Key finding" section).
- Key abstractions: Same `_meta.terminal_info` / `terminal_output` / `terminal_exit` keys, same `clientCapabilities._meta.terminal_output` capability gate, same provider-neutral terminal presentation in `dsh-tools`.
- Data flow: Same mapping chain (capability recorded at `initialize` → bash tool returns neutral presentation → bridge maps to `_meta` fields with `terminalId` from `callId`).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Same rejection of the client-side `terminal/create` sub-protocol with the same "forks execution into two backends" reasoning, and it preserves the observation that both reference agents keep agent-side execution (L20).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same ` ```console ` text fallback guarantee for clients without the capability; same reason the update's text `content` is omitted (L33).
- Surprising: Faithfully keeps the marker round-trip rationale (parse is the exact inverse of the emission, guarded by a round-trip test) and the note that the `dsh-tools` vocabulary must stay provider-neutral so no ACP type leaks in (L47).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-18-acp-terminal-and-tool-rendering.i18n.yaml
- Purpose: Bilingual-pair consistency record for the ACP terminal-rendering note — the git blob hashes of the EN and ZH sides.
- Key abstractions: two `path: sha1` lines under a header naming `pnpm run verify-translation-pairing --write`.
- Data flow: The translation verifier compares the recorded hashes against the working tree to detect a one-sided edit.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The header states both languages carry equal authority, so the pairing record is regenerated by tooling rather than hand-maintained.
- Edge cases / failure handling: A mismatched hash is the failure signal; the file carries no prose of its own.

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-18-compaction-capability-seam.md
- Purpose: The core context-compaction design: how a long conversation is summarised, where the summary lands in the message projection, and how compaction is prevented from corrupting the log.
- Key abstractions: `CompactionEngine` (interface, `ctx.compaction`) with three *abstract* ops `compactIfNeeded` / `compactNow` / `compactRegion`; `BasicCompactionEngine` (backend, owns the tail→head retention walk, `summarize()` hook, lock, overflow recovery); `dsh-compaction-tool-result-pruner`; `dsh-command-compact` (`/compact`); `ctx.tokenMeter`; the session *surface* projection with `surfaceOp: {op:'replace', startSeq, endSeq}` + `sourceEventSeqs`.
- Data flow: Pressure is measured at `agent/pre-step` **after** the previous response/tool-results/steering are durable and **before** the next request is derived; the backend summarises the older range via `ctx.llm.stream()` and appends a single `user/message` carrying `source: COMPACT_CHECKPOINT_SOURCE` + the replace op; `deriveMessages()` then yields `[summary_as_user_message, ...retained_entries]`. The `compaction/*` events are log-only bookkeeping.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The interface deliberately breaks the "Service Definition depends only on cordis" rule because its verbs act on a `Session` and its output uses `ContentBlock` — argued as "the contract's domain", not a coupling smell (L27–31). The summary rides on a `user/message` rather than a bespoke `compaction/*` event because `SurfaceEventType` is closed to message-producing types; the note insists "a summary genuinely *is* user-role context" (L87). The summariser **replays the routed request's prefix and appends the compaction directive as a trailing user message so the provider's warm KV cache is reused** (L37). All three ops are abstract so no strategy is baked into the contract (L35). `GenerateOptions.purpose = 'compaction'` is a typed discriminant rather than a `compact` boolean or an untyped metadata bag (L37, L116).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Retention keeps the smallest tail of *whole* surface units and expands until the cut is **tool-pairing balanced** — checked on surface order, not log sequence, because replacement summaries carry new sequence numbers at old surface positions; `compactRegion` refuses to split a tool call from its result (L59). An open tail step with no tool results yet makes compaction decline (`null`) and retry once it closes (L61). A dangling `compaction/start` after the newest `session/end-seed` is the live lock (busy); a *newer* `session/end-seed` proves the older unmatched start is stale, so resume/fork are not wedged by a dead writer (L104–105). A recoverable failure makes exactly one `compaction/end {error}` attempt and leaves the surface unchanged; if that append fails the unmatched start keeps blocking (L106).
- Surprising: The lock is released **last**, deliberately — releasing it first would turn the crash window from a *detectable orphan* into silent corruption (L97). Recovery returns `{kind:'retry'}` only if `session.surface.replaceGeneration` actually increased, so a no-op compaction cannot loop (L43). The result carries `llmStreamCall: true` because retained `rawOutput` alone does not prove which call path produced it (L37). Core `interruptedTurnClosers` is deliberately never taught about `compaction/*` (L110). Some single-unit overflow (an oversized indivisible `user/message`) is explicitly out of scope (L63).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-18-compaction-capability-seam.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-06-18-compaction-capability-seam.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the compaction capability-seam note (same 135 lines, same event-order diagram at L45–53 and L77–85).
- Key abstractions: Same four packages, same three abstract operations, same `ctx.tokenMeter` split, same `COMPACT_CHECKPOINT_SOURCE` marker and `toolPairingBalancedBefore/After` helpers.
- Data flow: Same pre-step pressure path and same "one `user/message` carries the summary" replacement; the translated event ladder keeps the `compaction/start → compaction/summary → user/message → compaction/end` order verbatim.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Same justification for the `dsh-session`/`dsh-llm` dependency deviation (L27–31) and same KV-cache-reuse rule — the summariser replays the routed request prefix and appends the directive as a trailing user message so the provider's warm cache is reused (L37).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same tool-pairing-balance retention, same open-tail refusal, same current-vs-later lifecycle classification of a dangling `compaction/start` (L104–105).
- Surprising: Preserves the two subtlest points verbatim — the lock is released last so a crash yields a detectable orphan rather than silent corruption (L97), and `llmStreamCall: true` is set only when exactly one call went through this context's LLM service (L37).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-18-compaction-capability-seam.i18n.yaml
- Purpose: Bilingual-pair consistency record for the compaction-seam note — EN and ZH git blob hashes.
- Key abstractions: two `path: sha1` lines; header names the `pnpm run verify-translation-pairing --write` re-record command, here with the note path as an argument.
- Data flow: Feeds the translation-pairing verifier that fails on a one-sided edit.
- Non-obvious design decisions: "Both languages carry equal authority" is stated in the header, so the Chinese side must be edited in lockstep rather than regenerated from the English.

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-21-subagent-capability-seam.md
- Purpose: Defines the subagent capability seam — the contract that lets an agent delegate work to a child agent over several interchangeable transports.
- Key abstractions: `SubagentRuntime` (`ctx.subagents`) with a **named-provider registry**; `SubagentProvider`, `SubagentRun`; the async `start(request) → Promise<SubagentRun>` primitive; three-package split (interface `dsh-subagent` / providers / consumer `dsh-tool-subagent`).
- Data flow: The model calls the `subagent` tool; the consumer binds to one provider by name; `start()` publishes a child and transfers a run handle; prompt/turn/cancel/infra outcomes settle through `run.result`; the parent log records only the spawn `tool/call` and its `tool/result` — child steps and tool calls stay outside the parent log (L58).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The seam is a **registry, not a single service**, because multiple implementations must coexist — the note explicitly mirrors the LLM adapter registry (`LlmRuntime.registerAdapter`) rather than the one-`ShellExecutor`-per-context bash seam, which throws on a second load (L25). Start-time capabilities (`agentOptions`, `outputSchema`, `depthLimit`, `toolFilter`, `persona`) are checked **before** delegating and rejected **loud** with `UNSUPPORTED_CAPABILITY` — never accepted-then-ignored (L49). Continuable creation is discovered by the *presence* of the optional `prepareContinuable` method, so TypeScript narrowing is the discovery mechanism and no separate capability flag can drift from the implementation (L50). Fresh vs forked are **separate backends, not a request flag**, and a fork seeds a balanced prefix of only *completed* parent turns — the in-flight turn is excluded because its subagent call has no result yet and cannot form valid replay history (L54).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A rejected `start()` cleans unpublished resources and emits **no** lifecycle event; a post-publication failure closes the published lifecycle pair and never hides the child id; non-completed outcomes become error results rather than successful partial output, with the safe provider diagnostic presented separately from partial assistant text; result and disposal rejections stay independently observable (L45, L62).
- Surprising: The *service* holds the multi-provider registry but the *tool* picks one — the model-facing schema carries **no** subagent transport/type parameter, and to expose several transports you load the tool plugin more than once with distinct `toolName`s (the registry rejects duplicate names) (L66). Recursion is bounded by an optional absolute depth limit plus a scoped `toolFilter`; the ACP backend advertises both off and rejects such requests (L74).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-22-acp-subagent-backend.md
- Purpose: The first out-of-process subagent backend — the harness acting as an ACP *client* that spawns a child agent process, proving the subagent seam generalises across a process boundary.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-subagent-acp` registering a `SubagentProvider`; one ACP session per run (`initialize` → `newSession` → `prompt`); a minimal client stub advertising no `fs`/`terminal` capabilities; a total `StopReason` → `SubagentStopReason` map.
- Data flow: Each `start` spawns a fresh subprocess; `session/update` notifications are consumed and `agent_message_chunk` text is accumulated as the result output (thoughts and tool-call cards are dropped); `session/request_permission` is auto-answered by a configured policy (`reject` declines everything, `allow` approves via the first allow-shaped option) so no prompt reaches a human; `dispose` kills the subprocess and awaits its exit.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The child's cwd is an **explicit resolution**, never the harness `process.cwd()` — deployment override (made absolute against the launch directory, validated at load) else the parent session header's cwd (validated at start), with a loud rejection before anything spawns when neither exists; one ACP server serves sessions from many workspaces, so `process.cwd()` cannot stand in for a session's workspace (L30). The candidate must be a directory the harness can *enter* (`X_OK`), because `statSync().isDirectory()` alone accepts a mode-600 directory that `spawn` would fail on with EACCES (L30). All start-time capabilities are `false`: an out-of-process child cannot honour the parent's `maxDepth` (no access to `parent.options.subagentDepth`) or `toolFilter` (it owns its own registry), so the service rejects such a request before `start` (L26).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Spawn/initialize/session-creation failures **reject `start()`** before publication after provider-owned cleanup; prompt/RPC/transport failures after publication settle `result` as `error` (or `aborted` after local cancellation), and `result` **never rejects** on a child-level failure (L34). A spawn-level `error` event (e.g. ENOENT for a bad command) is captured and raced against the ACP drive, so a bad command settles `error` instead of crashing the parent with an unhandled error (L38). A missing command and a torn pipe are both covered by the keyless suite (L42).
- Surprising: The child's environment is **credential-scrubbed by default** — ambient vars matching `/KEY|PASSWORD|SECRET|TOKEN/i` are not forwarded, and the child's own key is supplied *explicitly* through `config.env` layered **after** the scrub, so an intended `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY` survives while an incidental `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` does not (L38). `max_turn_requests` maps to `error` because it has no clean equivalent — the task did not finish (L34).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-24-workspace-context.md
- Purpose: Decides how repository guidance files (`AGENTS.md` / `CLAUDE.md`) enter a session's effective context — initial baseline injection, later deltas, precedence, and byte budgeting.
- Key abstractions: `packages/context/agent-instructions` (`@deepseek-ai/dsh-agent-instructions`) as a *request-context extension* (not a core service, not an fs backend); ordered `instructionFileCandidates` (default `['AGENTS.md','CLAUDE.md']`) and additive `localInstructionFileCandidates` (default `['AGENTS.local.md','CLAUDE.local.md']`); the fixed user-global file `$DSH_HOME/AGENTS.md` (default `~/.dsh`); a `maxBytes` deployment budget.
- Data flow: At the first `agent/pre-step` the plugin composes one sourced user-role baseline (user-global file → project root found by walking up to a root marker, default `.git` → candidates from root down to cwd) and folds it into the final claimed first-step batch right after the prompt so it becomes durable in the same request; later, a successful first-party `read`/`write`/`edit` triggers reconciliation of the touched descendant chain and queues an `Additional instructions from: <path>` system-reminder for the next request (L32, L42).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Only the **first existing** candidate in a directory loads, and `AGENTS.md` is an ordinary first candidate rather than a hidden priority — repos in transition commonly duplicate guidance across both files, and loading both would double it (L24, L74). State is recovered from **persisted JSON metadata** `{action, scope, path, digest?}` (SHA-1 over content) rather than by parsing rendered headings or hidden comments, because instruction prose can contain the same text and cause silent false positives (L52, L76). There is **no filesystem watcher** — detection happens at the next successful structured touch, resume reconciliation, or restoration of a shadowed baseline, which keeps the design deterministic and provider-neutral (L60, L86). The model-facing frame is the familiar user-role `<system-reminder>` rather than a harness-specific XML vocabulary, and a literal `</system-reminder>` anywhere in content or model-visible path/scope/budget metadata is escaped at the final rendering boundary **before** byte accounting completes (L38).
- Edge cases / failure handling: `maxBytes` applies separately to a rendered baseline or one dynamic batch and there is no implicit/unbounded budget; non-positive or non-finite values disable loading; when content exceeds the budget, **broader files are omitted before the most-specific file is truncated**, and a visible `Workspace instruction budget ...` notice names omitted and truncated paths (L64). `maxSourceBytes` is a per-file cap (1 MiB default) checked *before* reading, and content is still consumed through `streamText()` with a running UTF-8 byte count so missing/stale metadata cannot force an unbounded allocation (L66). A resolve/stat exception is classified as *unavailable* — it skips only that candidate and is never read as a deletion of an already-loaded scope (L20). A provider failure produces no removal; absence is accepted only when all configured candidates in that scope were probed successfully (L60). A removal is a tombstone, so a reappearing candidate becomes a new `set` (L56). Suppression state is valid only while the instruction event remains in the *visible* surface — if compaction shadows it, that state no longer suppresses a later load (L56, L58).
- Surprising: The plugin deliberately keeps **no process-wide cache and never retains instruction prose** — only `{path, version, digest}` per scope in a `WeakMap<Session, Map<scope, state>>`; provider versions are only an in-memory invalidation fast path while SHA-1 is the persisted cross-provider identity (L66). Repository text is explicitly treated as untrusted input: lower-authority user-role framing, precedence language and delimiter escaping reduce but do not eliminate prompt injection, and following a candidate symlink widens the surface to off-tree content, so the sandbox/permission layers are the real boundary (L84). Shell commands are **not** discovery triggers — inferring reached paths from a command string would require shell semantics the plugin does not own (L48).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-21-subagent-capability-seam.i18n.yaml
- Purpose: EN/ZH blob-hash pairing record for the subagent-seam note.
- Key abstractions: two `path: sha1` lines; header names `pnpm run verify-translation-pairing --write` with this note's path.
- Data flow: Consumed by the translation-pairing verifier to fail on a one-sided edit.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The header asserts both languages carry equal authority, so the ZH side must be edited in lockstep and the hashes re-recorded by tooling.
- Edge cases / failure handling: Hash mismatch is the sole failure signal; the file is machine-written and carries no prose.
- Surprising: The 77-line English note and its 77-line Chinese twin are pinned by 6 lines of YAML, so a translation drift is a CI-detectable event rather than a review-time accident.

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-22-acp-subagent-backend.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-06-22-acp-subagent-backend.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the out-of-process ACP subagent provider (same 64 lines).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-25-ask-user-question.md
- Purpose: Introduces a structured way for a model to pause and ask the human — the `ask_user_question` tool, its provider-neutral vocabulary, and the Web/TUI answer providers.
- Key abstractions: `dsh-user-interaction` (interface, `ctx.userInteraction`) colocated under `packages/ui` with the consumer `dsh-tool-ask-user`; `UserInteractionError extends HarnessError`; the request shape `questions: [{id, question, header?, options?: [{label, description?}], multi_select?}]`; the answer shape `{answers: [{id, selected, custom?}]}`.
- Data flow: The tool registers `ask_user_question`, forwards `{questions, agent, signal}` to the mounted provider, and returns the provider-computed structured answers as the tool result; the Web provider keeps a process-memory pending table keyed by a host-minted `rpcId`, broadcasts `question/requested`, and broadcasts `question/resolved` after answer/cancel/abort/disposal.
- Non-obvious design decisions: `selected` is **always an array**, so single-select and multi-select share one result shape, and `custom` (free-text "Other") overrides any selections with `selected` empty (L20). A provider that supports partial completion represents a deliberately skipped item with the *existing* `{id, selected: []}` shape rather than extending the vocabulary (L20). `label` doubles as the display text *and* the value returned to the model — there is no separate `value`, `recommended`, `allow_custom` or `desc` alias (L18). No loop-level pause primitive was added: a tool call already has the right async shape, and a new loop special case would make every loop implementation learn a UI concern (L42). The tool lives in `packages/ui`, not `packages/core`, because asking a human is a UI-backed product affordance (L38).
- Edge cases / failure handling: `NO_PROVIDER`, `ASK_ABORTED` and missing request ownership survive `ctx.tools.execute()` as machine-routable `{name, code}` tool errors, so "user cancelled" is distinguishable from a generic throw (L22). The Web provider **registers the wait before broadcasting** `question/requested`, replays the same id on every mux reopen, validates the session and the complete answer batch before claiming, and deletes the entry synchronously on claim so the first valid response wins and duplicate or late responses return `not-pending` (L26). The close control rejects the whole tool call with `ASK_CANCELLED`; per-item skip preserves earlier drafts (L28).
- Surprising: The Web composer is **IME-aware** — Enter during IME composition only confirms the input candidate rather than submitting (L28). A recommendation badge is shown but never auto-selects (L28). The TUI queues batched and simultaneous requests so one overlay owns keyboard focus at a time (L30).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-29-todo-write-tool.md
- Purpose: Adds the model-facing `todo_write` tool and decides where the task list lives — as event-sourced session state rather than an in-memory service.
- Key abstractions: `todo_write(todos: [{content, status}])`; the `todo/write` variant of `SessionEventMap` owned by the todo package; `status: pending | in_progress | completed`; the three-state triple shared with codex `update_plan` and the (now retired) ACP `PlanEntryStatus`.
- Data flow: The model sends the **entire** list every call and the new list replaces the old (last-write-wins on replay); the list is appended as a `todo/write` event carrying the full `{todos}` snapshot; a reopened session re-derives the standing plan from the latest `todo/write` not followed by a later `turn/start`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: State lives on the **event log** because the harness is already event-sourced — durability, replay and resume reconstruction come free, and an in-memory `ctx.todos` service "would have to reinvent all of that" (L22). `todo/write` is deliberately **excluded from `SurfaceEventType`**: it produces no conversation message, so it never joins the ordered surface and never reaches `deriveMessages()` — a todo update never perturbs derived model history (L26). The item shape is the minimum `{content, status}`: no `activeForm`, no id (whole-list replace needs no stable identity), no priority — "each dropped field is one less thing the model must produce on every call" (L30).
- Edge cases / failure handling: `execute` rejects empty or duplicate `content` and, when `allowParallelInProgress` is false, more than one active task; a rejected write returns an `isError` result so the model self-corrects; non-agent calls are rejected outright (L34, L38). The dev-mode invariant still requires the event to sit inside an open turn, which it always does because it is appended mid-step (L26).
- Surprising: A `SessionEventMap` variant produces **no cordis-catalog row** (the generator only scans `interface Events` declarations) and therefore legitimately carries **no `@mode` tag** — adding one would be meaningless (L42). Single owner, no swarm machinery: there is no shared scope, resolver or delta protocol, because cross-agent lists would need per-item log deltas and explicit scope selection (L34).

- Non-obvious design decisions: Equal authority for both languages is stated in the header, so the Chinese side is edited in lockstep.

- Surprising: The note deliberately drops fields the references carry, and this pairing file is the only machine record tying the two language versions together.

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-hook-bridges.md
- Purpose: Introduces `dsh-hooks-claude-code` and `dsh-hooks-codex` — adapters that let existing Claude Code / Codex shell-hook configs run unmodified against the harness's typed interception points.
- Key abstractions: the typed interception points (`agent/session-start`, `agent/pre-step`, `tools/pre-execute`, `tools/post-execute`, `agent/turn-stopping`, `subagent/start`, `subagent/end`); the shared wire library `dsh-hook-protocol` (`runHook`, `mergeHookOutputs`, `MergedHookOutcome`); the two dialect-specific payload shapes.
- Data flow: Each bridge parses the external config once at load, picks a matcher mode, builds a per-event stdin payload, calls the shared lib, and maps the neutral outcome onto the extension point's typed Decision (deny→reject / deny / block, context-only→delegate+fold, CC `ask`→the real approval seam).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The stated rule is "**a bridge is a compatibility adapter, not a power tool**" — anything a bridge can do a native cordis plugin does more powerfully (typed returns, full `ctx`, no serialization boundary) (L12). Every injected context is explicitly labelled `{kind: 'plugin', plugin: 'hooks-claude-code'|'hooks-codex'}` so the resulting `user/message.source` is the plugin and **not the user** — the "mislabel guard" (L39). A context-only hook is **not** treated as a veto: returning `enter` from a waterfall listener *without* calling `next()` would short-circuit every later listener, so each bridge delegates via `next()` **before** prepending its context, and only a real `deny`/`block` short-circuits (L45). Hooks are run **serially** rather than concurrently, deliberately, to keep each hook's `hook/invoked`/`hook/result` pair adjacent and deterministic; the decision fold itself is order-independent (`deny > ask > allow`) so the outcome still matches (L69).
- Edge cases / failure handling: The config is parsed **once at load**; a read/parse failure logs and registers nothing rather than crashing boot — "a typo'd path must not take the agent down" (L53). Only shell-form `type: 'command'` hooks run for CC; `http`/`mcp_tool`/`prompt`/`agent` are parsed-and-skipped; Codex skips `async: true` and non-command entries. Emit-listener paths run detached with their `inject` inside a `.catch` that logs, so a throwing inject cannot break session boot or the loop. A CC `ask` result without an ApprovalService or answerer **fails closed to `deny`** (L35).
- Surprising: `CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR` defaults per-run to the agent's session workspace (`session.header.cwd`) when `config.projectDir` is omitted, so a stock project-relative hook works in the default setup (L49). The note names its own compatibility gaps with TODOs: `updatedInput` is logged+warned but not honoured; `stop_hook_active` is always reported `false` (so a Stop hook that unconditionally blocks force-continues every step); `continue:false` is folded into the shared outcome but no bridge acts on it because the interception points have no "hard-halt the agent" primitive (L61–63). The two bridges also differ in wire details: CC stdin carries a trailing newline, Codex's is written **without** one (L18–19).

- Key abstractions: two `path: sha1` lines and the shared pairing header.

- Non-obvious design decisions: The header's "both languages carry equal authority" is the reason a 73-line design note must be maintained twice.
- Edge cases / failure handling: Only a hash mismatch can fail; nothing else in the file is load-bearing.
- Surprising: The note's own "bridge is an adapter, not a power tool" rule applies to this file too — it records state, it does not enforce it.
### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-hook-bridges.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-06-30-hook-bridges.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the CC/Codex hook bridge decision (same 73 lines, same Outcome→Decision table).
- Key abstractions: Same two bridge plugins, same shared `dsh-hook-protocol` library primitives (`runHook`, `mergeHookOutputs`, `MergedHookOutcome`), same typed Decision vocabulary.
- Data flow: Same pipeline (parse config → matcher mode → per-event stdin payload → shared lib → typed Decision), same CC-trailing-newline vs Codex-no-newline wire difference.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Keeps the "**桥接是兼容性适配器，不是高级工具**" rule and the mislabel guard that every injected context is `{kind:'plugin'}` so the message source is the plugin and not the user (L12, L39).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same "config parsed once at load; parse failure logs and registers nothing instead of crashing boot", same fail-closed `deny` when no ApprovalService/answerer exists (L35, L53).
- Surprising: Preserves the waterfall short-circuit hazard (returning `enter` without `next()` hides later policy/sandbox listeners) and the deliberate *serial* hook execution with its latency cost (L45, L69).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-hook-protocol-lib.md
- Purpose: Defines `dsh-hook-protocol` — the shared wire-protocol core both hook bridges build on, extracted because Codex reimplements a *subset* of the Claude Code hook protocol.
- Key abstractions: `matcherDiagnostic(pattern, mode)` / `matchesMatcher(pattern, query, mode)`; `runHook(bash, hook, options)`; `parseHookOutput(exit, stdout, stderr)`; `mergeHookOutputs(outputs)` → `MergedHookOutcome`; the `hook/invoked` / `hook/result` session events with `appendHookInvoked` / `appendHookResult` helpers.
- Data flow: A bridge parses its config atomically → builds its dialect payload → `runHook` serialises it to stdin and executes it through the `ctx.shell` seam → `parseHookOutput` decodes exit code + stdout into a neutral `HookOutput` → `mergeHookOutputs` folds matched hooks into the most-restrictive outcome → the bridge maps that onto its typed Decision and appends `hook/*`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The single axis the dialects differ on (matcher semantics) is collapsed into one `mode` parameter, so the shared library stays dialect-neutral (L19). `runHook` deliberately goes through `ctx.shell` rather than a bespoke `spawn` because that executor already provides the scrubbed-but-overridable env, process-group kills and timeout the protocol needs — and `dsh-shell`'s `stdin`/`env` fields exist as the trusted-plugin API for exactly this (L20). Fields with **no consumer on any path are not parsed at all** (CC's `suppressOutput` is skipped because hook stdout never enters a transcript) (L21). `appendHookResult` owns the durable record's semantics — the decision string and the 500-character `stderrSummary` truncation are derived from the `HookOutput` in the library, not re-implemented per bridge (L23).
- Edge cases / failure handling: An invalid regex in a *runnable* group makes the whole config load fail with a stable dialect/pattern/event diagnostic and **no hook listeners registered**; at runtime an invalid regex is contained as a non-match so a direct library caller never throws into the agent loop (L19). `runHook` never throws — an executor rejection becomes a non-blocking-error `HookOutput` (L20). Exit `0` → lenient JSON parse; exit `2` → blocking error surfaced as `decision:'block'` so no caller needs a separate exit-code branch; anything else → non-blocking error (L21).
- Surprising: A **library, not a plugin** — it registers and injects nothing (L12); the merge precedence is `deny > ask > allow` with halt sticky on the first `continue:false`, block reasons joined by `\n\n`, and contexts accumulated in order (L22). The `hook/*` events are log-only like `compaction/*` and deliberately *not* `SurfaceEventType`s (L23).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-hook-protocol-lib.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-06-30-hook-protocol-lib.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the shared hook protocol library decision (same 33 lines).
- Key abstractions: Same four primitive families (matcher / execution / decode / merge) plus the `hook/*` session events, and the same shared-vs-per-dialect split.
- Data flow: Same chain from config parse through `runHook` → `parseHookOutput` → `mergeHookOutputs` → typed Decision → `hook/*` append.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Keeps the founding observation that Codex's engine reads the same `hooks.json` and names itself after Claude's, diverging only intentionally — which is the justification for extracting a shared core at all (L10); preserves the "one `mode` parameter absorbs the only dialect difference" design (L19).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same "invalid regex ⇒ whole-config load failure, no listeners registered" rule, and the same containment of an invalid regex as a runtime non-match so a direct library caller never throws into the loop (L19).
- Surprising: Faithfully preserves the "fields with no consumer on any path are not parsed at all" rule and the reason (`suppressOutput` is meaningless because hook stdout never reaches a transcript) (L21), plus the 500-character `stderrSummary` truncation owned centrally by `appendHookResult` (L23).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-hook-protocol-lib.i18n.yaml
- Purpose: EN/ZH blob-hash pairing record for the hook-protocol library note.
- Key abstractions: two `path: sha1` lines and the shared pairing header.
- Data flow: Read by the translation-pairing verifier.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Both languages are declared equal-authority, so the note is maintained twice rather than generated.
- Edge cases / failure handling: Only a hash mismatch can fail; nothing in the file is load-bearing at runtime.
- Surprising: The library itself exists to avoid duplicating a protocol between two dialects, and this pairing file is the smallest possible instance of the same "one source of truth per artefact" discipline.

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-interception-extension-points.md
- Purpose: Defines the typed-Decision interception surface a hook or plugin programs against — the canonical lifecycle event API the bridges merely translate onto.
- Key abstractions: `agent/created`, `agent/pre-step(...) → PreStepDecision`, `agent/turn-stopping`; the five-phase tool pipeline `tools/pre-execute` → guards → `tools/execute` → dispatch → `tools/post-execute` → `ToolDefinition.finalizeContent` → `tools/result`; `ctx.tools.guard()`; `ToolRunContext.deferContext()`.
- Data flow: The registry snapshots the caller's input, materialises and freezes the arguments, assigns an opaque token and snapshots the visible definition's finalize callback *before* policy begins; nested calls carry only the parent token; only `signal` may change around dispatch, so the log, UI and tool body agree on what ran (L26). `tools/result` fires after every transform, lossless-JSON materialisation and the outer error boundary.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The reframe is that "**native hooks are not a package**" — a native hook is just an ordinary Cordis plugin, so the real product is a powerful, well-typed canonical event API and the bridges are translators (L10). The surface deliberately gives each phase **one kind of authority**: hooks return decisions, execution wrappers wrap, terminal guards only deny, final observers only observe (L59). `ctx.tools.guard()` may deny or abstain but never force-allow, so listener ordering cannot resurrect an operation a final invariant forbids (L29). `tools/post-execute` value replacement revalidates and recomputes the presentation, while **content replacement preserves the programmatic value and is explicitly not a confidentiality boundary** (L31). `ToolDefinition.finalizeContent` is snapshotted with the visible definition at call creation, runs exactly once after the registry has losslessly snapshotted the candidate outcome, and may replace `content` but cannot rewrite `isError`, structured error identity, contexts or presentation metadata (L32).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Every resolved pre-execute decision still reaches post-policy, and a throwing listener becomes a final normalised failure (L28). An `ask` decision resolves through the optional approval seam — only `allowed-once` proceeds; rejection, cancellation, an unavailable channel, a missing approval service or an agent-less call all normalise to a denial (L28). Observer failures in `tools/result` are contained per listener and cannot change or reject `ToolRuntime.execute()`'s returned outcome (L33). Core dispatch and the tool body sit inside normalisation boundaries, so tool/listener/invalid-value/renderer/non-JSON/identity-shape failures resolve as JSON-safe `isError` results instead of escaping the turn (L35).
- Surprising: Pre-step policy runs at **every proposed step**, and a tool continuation with no newly claimed input still submits an *empty* batch so per-request context producers can add logged messages to that exact request (L39). Post-tool `additionalContexts` and async injections enter the active-batch FIFO and are appended only when the batch settles — appending immediately would interleave `result(c1) → context → result(c2)` and break tool-call/result adjacency; an accepted outer call keeps deferred contexts *before* decision contexts, while an outer block discards them (L41). Pre-tool **input rewrite is out of scope** because history, the audit call and UI presentation all read arguments logged before execution (L47).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-interception-extension-points.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-06-30-interception-extension-points.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the typed-Decision interception surface (same 60 lines, same three "load-bearing loop decisions").
- Key abstractions: Same agent events, same five-phase tool pipeline, same typed Decision unions and `ToolRunContext.deferContext()` buffering.
- Data flow: Same registry-sealing order (snapshot input, freeze arguments, assign token, snapshot finalize callback) and the same rule that only `signal` may change around dispatch (L26).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "native hooks are not a package" reframe and the per-phase single-authority rule (L10, L59), and the guard rule that a guard can deny or abstain but never force-allow (L29).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same normalisation of every failure mode to a JSON-safe `isError` and same per-listener containment of observer failures (L35).
- Surprising: Keeps the subtle FIFO rule (deferred contexts before decision contexts on accept; discarded on block) and the "empty batch on a tool continuation" rule that lets per-request context producers target one exact request (L39, L41).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-session-store-fork-api.md
- Purpose: Defines the live-session fork API — how a session is branched at a safe boundary, and why a fork must be rejected rather than silently clipped when the boundary is invalid.
- Key abstractions: `SessionStore.fork(source: Session | SessionId, boundary?: SessionSeq, childSessionId?: SessionId): Session`; `SessionForkSource`; the child metadata `parentSession`, logical `isSeeded: true` and the separate exact `inheritedEventCount`.
- Data flow: `boundary` is the branded **inclusive** source event `seq` to copy through and defaults to the source's last event (an empty source with no boundary creates an empty child); the store validates that the boundary exists and that the prefix's latest turn boundary is not an unmatched `turn/start`; the prefix is deep-cloned into the child seed; the child inherits the source `cwd` and is stamped with lineage.
- Non-obvious design decisions: A general session fork must **either fork the requested boundary or reject it — never silently clip**. Silently clipping to the last completed boundary is correct for `dsh-subagent-fork-in-process` (delegation often starts mid-turn) but wrong for user-initiated branching because it hides that the requested fork point was invalid and silently drops the parent turn tail (L12, L44). The API lives directly on `ctx.sessions` with **no separate `ctx.sessionFork` service** — an earlier iteration shipped one and it "overfit the capability-seam pattern" (no swappable backend, no extra event surface, no independent ownership lifecycle) (L16, L40). A single `fork()` with an explicit `boundary` was preferred over a `snapshot()` + `fork()` pair because the only supported consumer creates a session immediately (L42).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Typed errors distinguish missing sources, stale objects, duplicate child ids, invalid boundaries and prefixes ending during execution; an empty prefix is forkable (L30). The Host RPC accepts `atSeq` as an *anchor within the desired turn* and selects the first `turn/end` at or after it, but an anchor already in the log with no matching `turn/end` returns `fork-unavailable` and **never falls back to an earlier turn**, so a message action cannot silently omit the clicked message (L34). A workspace-attachment failure returns `workspace-attach-failed` **with the already-published child id**, and the client reconciles that child into its summary list before surfacing the error (L36).
- Surprising: The Host installs the latest logged provider, model and reasoning target on the child *before* it can run — pre-publication setup (L36). Persistence needed no new machinery: a forked child starts life with seeded events so the JSONL backend persists the seed once and encodes the exact cut separately from the logical `isSeeded` flag (L48).

- Data flow: Same inclusive `boundary` defaulting to the source's last event, same validation that the prefix's latest turn boundary is not an unmatched `turn/start`, same deep-clone into the child seed.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the core rule that a general session fork must either fork the requested boundary or reject it, never silently clip (L12) — with the same reasoning that clipping is correct for `dsh-subagent-fork-in-process` but wrong for user-initiated branching because it hides an invalid fork point and drops the parent turn tail (L44).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same typed error taxonomy and the same `fork-unavailable` rule: an anchor in the log with no following `turn/end` never falls back to an earlier turn (L34); same `workspace-attach-failed` carrying the already-published child id so the client can reconcile it first (L36).
- Surprising: Keeps the rejection of a separate `ctx.sessionFork` service as "overfitting the capability-seam pattern" and the single-`fork()`-over-`snapshot()`+`fork()` argument (L40, L42).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-subagent-observe-enrich.md
- Purpose: Adds one field — `lastAssistantMessage` — to the `subagent/end` lifecycle payload so a hooks bridge can report what a subagent produced without reaching for the live run.
- Key abstractions: `SubagentRunEndInfo.lastAssistantMessage`; the existing `subagent/start` / `subagent/end` emits; the readonly typed `SubagentResult.output`.
- Data flow: On the settle path the field *is* the child's readonly `SubagentResult.output`; on an infrastructure rejection where no `SubagentResult` exists it is absent and the event reports `stopReason: 'error'`. `SubagentService.start()` attaches result observation to the ready provider run, emits `subagent/start`, then returns the run.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The enrichment is deliberately **observe-only** — no control-flow change and no waterfall — because a control-flow `subagent/end` would require reshaping it from emit to waterfall, restructuring `start` to await listeners, and implementing a `resume` capability in the in-process provider (L28). An `agentType` subagent-kind label was **dropped in review** as a Claude-Code concept nothing in the harness interprets; the CC bridge instead feeds Claude Code's own default matcher value `"general-purpose"`, so the harness ships exactly one enrichment (L22).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A rejected provider start emits neither event; per-listener containment keeps one bad subscriber from stranding a live run or starving later listeners (L18).
- Surprising: An in-process listener can reach the published child via `ctx.agents.get(info.id)` while a remote provider need not have a local registry entry at all (L18); no snapshot or e2e change was needed because the events fire exactly as before with one more optional field (L32).

- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "one enrichment only" decision and the reason the `agentType` label was dropped (a Claude-Code concept, with the CC bridge supplying `"general-purpose"` itself) (L22).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same "a rejected start emits neither event" rule and the same per-listener containment (L18).
- Surprising: Keeps the `FIXME(subagent-continuation)` / `TODO` anchors that mark where a future control-flow version would land if the background/steering subagent redesign happens (L28).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-05-dynamic-workflows.md
- Purpose: Introduces script-driven multi-agent orchestration — the model writes a JavaScript orchestration script and a runtime executes it, so the script rather than the conversation holds the loop, branching and intermediate results.
- Key abstractions: `ctx.workflowEngine` (abstract `WorkflowEngine`, bash-seam shape — one engine per context, **no** named-provider registry, because engines are deployment swaps not co-residents); the script API `agent(prompt, options)`, `parallel(thunks)`, `pipeline(items, ...stages)`, `phase(title)`, `log(message)`, `args`; the `workflow/*` observe-only events; `SubagentStartRequest.outputSchema`.
- Data flow: A workflow call carries JSON `meta` (name, description, optional `whenToUse`/`phases`) and a `script` body with top-level `await` returning a JSON value; the engine runs the VM inside a sandboxed PTC Node process; the host validates metadata and parses the body *before* publication and binds the guest to subagents and workflow observers; the tool starts, awaits, disposes in a `try/finally`, bridges `exec.signal`, and maps a non-`completed` outcome to `isError`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: **`meta` is data and is never evaluated** — Claude Code's exact `export const meta = {...}` format was rejected because obtaining meta would require evaluating model-written text on the host, and "even an empty timed vm context cannot bound script-controlled getters when the host reads the resulting object"; a JSON parameter removes the scanner, evaluation and host-spin holes at the cost of moving the meta header into the parameter (L68). Hook misuse — unknown/deferred options, malformed arguments, unsupported schema subsets, tripped caps, seam start failures — throws a `WorkflowError` with `fatal: true` and the combinators **re-throw** rather than nulling the item, because "a typo'd option dissolves into a `null` indistinguishable from a child failure — the accepted-then-ignored failure mode this repo bans" (L20). The engine uses a shared **PTC Node process** rather than in-process `node:vm`, because a synchronous spin past the first await cannot be killed in-process (the vm `timeout` covers only the first slice) and `dispose()` could otherwise only abandon a script on the host loop (L64). `WorkflowRun.result` **never rejects** — failures resolve as `stopReason: 'error' | 'cancelled'` (L24).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Pipeline stages receive `(prev, item, index)` with no cross-stage barrier; a failed child or an ordinary stage error resolves the affected item to `null` and skips its remaining stages (L18). `start()` throws synchronously for a script that cannot begin. The workflow events carry **data snapshots** and `workflow/end` omits the result value, and are per-listener contained. The recording path observes rather than controls: its first append failure disables later writes for that run and leaves a legal prefix without changing the tool result (L38).
- Surprising: The **tool description is the model-facing authoring spec**, and the usage policy ships as the tool's own `tool:<toolName>` prompt section — "tool guidance lives in tool plugins, never in the deployment persona" (L36). Structured output works by giving each structured child its own scoped capture tool on `child.ctx`; a schema-valid committed capture becomes mandatory for successful completion, later side effects are denied once capture is pending, and the child stops without another model step after commit (L44). Provider JSON mode was rejected because it guarantees valid JSON, not schema conformance (L72).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-05-skill-system.md
- Purpose: Implements the skill pattern — keep the request prompt small by listing instruction bundles, then load the full body when the model decides a task matches.
- Key abstractions: `ctx.skills` as a pure provider registry; `dsh-skill-filesystem` as the local provider; `dsh-tool-skill` owning the durable session catalog and the model-facing `skill({name})` loader; the `skill_content` / `skill_resources` / `skill_instructions` result shape; `resourceBase`.
- Data flow: Providers register synchronously in `apply()`; the registry validates candidates, resolves same-name skills first-wins by rank → provider registration order → provider-local order, then sorts summaries by skill name for deterministic consumers. The local provider scans cwd-sensitive project roots, custom roots and user roots in first-wins rank order (project `.dsh`, project `.agents`, `customSkillDirs`, user `.dsh`, user `.agents`, skipping `.system`). The tool injects one durable user-role `<system-reminder>` catalog as a sourced `user/message` at the session's first `agent/pre-step`, and only when the agent's tool view resolves this plugin's exact `skill` registration.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The catalog contains **sorted name and description only** — it excludes bodies, paths, sources, providers and routing hints, and full bodies are never included (L28). Descriptions are whitespace-normalized, XML-escaped and capped by `catalogDescriptionMaxLength` (default 500, minimum 3) (L28). The registry **caches only completed catalog snapshots and retries when a provider/runtime revision changes during discovery**, so an unload cannot freeze a stale, unresolvable skill into a session catalog (L20). Local filesystem I/O goes through `ctx.fs` when a filesystem service is loaded (with Node fs only as a fallback for minimal contexts) so a remote filesystem can supply skills (L26). The provider registry mirrors the subagent seam: the registry owns conflict resolution and consumers, implementations own loading (L40).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Missing roots, unreadable or malformed skill files, and transient provider `list()` failures degrade to **warn-and-skip** so one bad source does not make every agent request fail — but malformed candidates still fail fast because they are provider contract violations (L26). An unresolved skill name reports "unknown or no longer available", while an invalid name and a skill with `invocation.modelInvocable: false` keep **distinct** tool errors (L30).
- Surprising: YAML frontmatter is parsed with the `yaml` package rather than `js-yaml` or a hand-written parser, because the accepted schema includes an open `metadata` object and a narrow parser would either reject valid YAML users expect or grow into an unreviewed YAML subset (L24, L48). The catalog moved out of the request-only session-prefix extension point into durable history after the unified sourced-message decision, keeping the same message shape (L28, L42).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-06-explicit-tool-order.md
- Purpose: Fixes model-facing tool order, which previously followed plugin registration order and therefore varied with a concurrent-import race — and order affects request bytes, caching and the durable header.
- Key abstractions: `toolOrder?: string[]` on `dsh-system-prompt`; the reserved rest entry `'<unlisted-tools>'` (`TOOL_ORDER_REST`); the system-prompt assembly as the owner of canonical tool order.
- Data flow: `assemble()` canonicalizes provider tools **before** the `system-prompt/assemble` waterfall, removing registration-order variance at its source; the waterfall starts from that deterministic list, and unchanged order flows into the request header, the frozen request and the reconstruction checks (L23).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The default (no `toolOrder`) is plain **lexicographic** name order using code-unit comparison so it is locale-independent and determinism needs no configuration (L21). A listed name with no registered tool is a **configuration error** that rejects every `assemble()` — chosen because `assemble()` is the earliest moment the registered tool set exists to check against and the only universal one (cordis has no "all plugins loaded" event, and registrations can change at any time) (L17). A boot-time validation pass was rejected because it would cost a public service method plus a structural coupling from generic boot glue to one service and could not replace the assembly-time check anyway (L38). The rest entry is mandatory exactly once and may not be a real tool's `ToolSchema.name` (L19, L20).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Because a schemastery array defaults to `[]`, an omitted `toolOrder` must stay **absent** rather than becoming an explicitly configured empty list (invalid — no rest entry), so every schema that accepts the field forces the default to `undefined` (L27). A misspelled or unloaded tool name fails the **turn**, not the boot: the turn closes balanced with an `error` reason, `agent/error` mirrors it, no step opens, no `request/header` is logged, no request reaches the adapter, and the agent returns to idle — the process stays up, matching the repo rule that explicit config references must not be silently ignored (L47). A provider returning the reserved rest-entry name fails in the same shape, so the sentinel can never become an ambiguous real tool (L48).
- Surprising: The scope is deliberately narrow — this fixes the *registration-order race*, not plugin behaviour; a `system-prompt/assemble` listener may still add, remove or rearrange tools and owns the determinism of what it emits (L25). The alternative "canonicalize in `llm.stream()`" was rejected because it runs *after* the header event is logged (the flake survives) and silently disarms the reconstruction invariant (L36).
### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-06-explicit-tool-order.i18n.yaml
- Purpose: Bilingual-pair consistency record for the explicit-tool-order note — the git blob hashes of its English and Chinese sides.
- Key abstractions: two `path: sha1` lines under a four-line header naming the `pnpm run verify-translation-pairing --write` repair command with this note's path as the argument.
- Data flow: The translation-pairing verifier reads the recorded hashes and compares them against the working tree, so a one-sided edit to either `.md` becomes a mechanical CI failure rather than a review-time accident.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The header states that both languages carry equal authority, so the Chinese side is a maintained twin to be edited in lockstep — not an artefact regenerated from the English.
- Edge cases / failure handling: A mismatched hash pair is the only failure mode; the file is machine-written and carries no prose of its own.
- Surprising: The note it pins decides that tool *order* is part of the request bytes and therefore of the cache prefix, and this 6-line file is the mechanism that keeps its two language versions from silently drifting apart.

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-06-sandbox.md
- Purpose: The subprocess sandbox design — a confinement seam with per-platform native runners, a one-shot escalation path, and per-session runtime modes, all composed from the leaf `cordis.yml` without touching the agent loop.
- Key abstractions: `ctx.sandbox` (`SandboxProvider.confine(argv, policy, signal?)` returning the argv to spawn instead of the caller's own); `SandboxMode` (`read-only` / `workspace-write` / `danger-full-access`, FILE effects only); `SandboxEnforcement` (`full`/`partial`); `ctx.sandboxPolicy.resolve()`; the `sessionProjections` registry with one projection unit per knob; `dsh-permission-presets`; `SANDBOX_UNAVAILABLE`.
- Data flow: A deployment composes four `cordis.yml` rows (sandbox provider, `dsh-bash-sandbox`, `dsh-user-approval`, `dsh-permission-presets`); the bash consumer awaits `ctx.sandbox` for the exact `['bash','-c',command]` argv, rechecks cancellation, and spawns the provider's result directly; policy resolves as explicit escalation mode > session override > configured default, with `SessionHeader.cwd` > configured fallback root; the knob's session event is folded by a projection unit and checkpointed, so `effective(session) = stateOf(session,'<knob>') ?? config default`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The escalation retry is a **new logged tool call**, not an auto-retry inside the same one — "one `tool/call` would have produced two executions with different policies", which the log cannot reconstruct (L152). The escalation fields are **capability-gated on the mounted executor**, because advertising an option the harness cannot honour manufactures doomed grants (L153). The escalation must be *strictly wider* than the session's effective mode; a same-mode request needs no approval and narrower or unsupported targets fail closed without prompting (L90). The **stable-system-prompt statement of the sandbox mode was shipped and then removed on live evidence**: with `Bash commands run under the "read-only" file sandbox.` in every request, the model refused to *attempt* denied-then-escalatable work — five of twelve turns in the first manual session ended with zero tool calls — turning the sandbox into a soft lockout (L159). Policy rides each **call**, not the provider, so two consumers may confine under different policies simultaneously and an escalated retry is a new call with a wider policy (L56). Hard-matching a retry to a prior denial was rejected — command-string identity is fragile, and the real boundary is the human seeing the command plus the justification (L156).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A denial returns a `[sandbox: file access denied under <mode> mode]` marker **as a result fact orthogonal to exit code** — the command RAN and the kernel refused the file effect (L193). A broken runner is told apart from a failing command only by an *attributable* `ENOENT`/`EACCES` for `argv[0]` plus a `runnerFailureRules` match (exit-code gate AND a fatal stderr line after exact informational exclusions); a runner failure outranks a denial, and a Landlock partial-enforcement notice plus an ordinary child failure stays a command result (L194). With no usable backend, `confine()` rejects with the fail-closed `SANDBOX_UNAVAILABLE` and the command never spawns — never a silent unconfined passthrough (L54, L195). A rejected escalation executes nothing and permits no re-ask; no grant is persisted. Older-ABI Landlock reports `enforcement: 'partial'` rather than refusing the host (L70, L202). A granted escalation is **not** a working sandbox: an unavailable backend still fails closed even for a granted confining mode, while a granted `danger-full-access` run never touches the provider at all (L187).
- Surprising: The runner attribution protocol is **in-band** (exit status + stderr), so a confined child can mimic a fatal runner line — accepted, because the child is already confined, and not a sandbox bypass (L183). The launcher is a ~300-line C11 program over the raw Landlock UAPI with a statically linked musl, installing the ruleset on itself and `exec`ing (rulesets survive `execve`), with `--probe` enforcing a maximal ruleset in a short-lived child so a *present but unusable* `bwrap` fails its probe and selection falls through to the packaged Landlock launcher (L66, L196). The whole policy exchange — context messages, headers, knob events, approvals and results — reconstructs from the session log alone, with no bookkeeping events beyond the two knob events (L169). A "broken sandbox" and a "failing command" have an FAQ entry each because confusing them is the operator's first question (L193–195).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-07-mcp-client-plugin.md
- Purpose: Adds an MCP client that registers external MCP server tools as native model-facing tools, with no per-server glue code.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-mcp-client` (one package, no capability-seam trio — "don't split preemptively"); the `transport`-discriminated config union (`stdio` | `streamable-http`) with `serverName` as the required local namespace; the `rawName` / `publicName` split; `publicToolName()` normalisation.
- Data flow: On connect the plugin lists tools with `listTools(undefined,{cacheMode:'refresh'})`, derives each `publicName`, and registers each as a raw `ToolDefinition` (the MCP JSON Schema and description pass through unchanged — only the model-facing name is replaced); the executor closes over `rawName` and calls the SDK's `callTool` with the model arguments, `exec.signal`, the timeout and the discovered definition.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The `mcp__<serverName>__<rawName>` shape is the de-facto standard among multi-server clients (Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, VS Code, Cline, Roo Code, Goose, OpenCode), and the always-on namespace makes cross-server collisions **structurally impossible** instead of handling them at collision time — a Microsoft survey of 1,470 servers found 775 colliding tool names (L98, L124). `serverName` is **local configuration, never the remote `serverInfo.name`**, because the remote name is untrusted, is not unique across deployments (prod and staging report the same name) and may change on server upgrade — none of which may silently rename model-facing tools (L58, L191). When normalisation or truncation changed the name, a **12-hex-char SHA-256 of the `(serverName, rawName)` identity is appended**, so distinct MCP identities can never collapse into one public name (L110). The public name is never sent to the server and is never parsed back to recover the raw name. The raw model-facing names plus an optional `toolPrefix` were rejected: the premise that servers already use semantic prefixes is false (the official GitHub server publishes bare `create_issue`) and collision-time prefixing makes the available tool set depend on plugin load order (L183).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A duplicate `serverName` across live instances is a configuration error — the later instance fails at load with an actionable message, never silent shadowing (L58). A server listing the same tool name twice is an invalid tool list: the sync throws and the **previous generation stays registered**; a registry conflict during the swap means a foreign tool is squatting on the namespace, so the partial generation is rolled back to zero tools from that server and logged loudly (L129–130). A failed re-sync preserves the previous tool generation. The stdio child environment is built from the shared `scrubbedParentEnv()` base (removing ambient `/KEY|PASSWORD|SECRET|TOKEN/i` and ambient `DSH_*` names) with `config.env` merged **on top**, so explicit overrides survive the scrub (L155). A disconnection triggers bounded-exponential-backoff reconnect with a per-outage attempt budget; exhaustion unregisters the server's tools and stops until reload.
- Surprising: The `mcp__` marker costs 5 characters on every name and is accepted because descriptions and JSON schemas dominate tool-definition tokens while the qualifier buys stable identity, collision isolation and MCP-wide policy shapes (`mcp__*`, `mcp__github__*`) (L221). Public names are part of session history and permission APIs, so changing the algorithm after release is a breaking change (L220). The canonical MCP value and a separate Native projection are kept apart because programmatic callers need protocol-complete blocks and `structuredContent` while Native consumers need durable images (L199).
