### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-07-plan-mode.md
- Purpose: Plan mode as a durable, logged, per-agent collaboration state — the model explores, produces a reviewable artifact, crosses an explicit approval boundary, and the state survives resume and fork.
- Key abstractions: `mode/set` as a log-only, non-surface `SessionEventMap` member with whole-value-replace semantics; the pure `foldMode(events)`; `ctx.modes` (`list()` / `get(agent)` / `set(agent, mode)`); the `ModeDefinition { section }` config shape; the `exit_plan_mode` tool; a `mode:policy` prompt section at order 50.
- Data flow: A mode flip records a pending intent in a `WeakMap` (it cannot append immediately because every session event is turn-enclosed), which contained listeners flush at `agent/prompt-submit`, `agent/turn-continuation` or a post-composed `agent/request-error` retry decision; the flush appends one coalesced `context/message` notice only when the flushed mode differs from the fold at the last `request/header`; the mode section then renders the active definition's guidance into the next request.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Modes and enforcement knobs are **separate axes that never read or write each other** — the mode is a collaboration stance, the sandbox mode and approval policy are enforcement, exactly as Codex keeps Plan/Default presets separate from sandbox and approval settings (L24, L144). Two enforcement shapes were **removed before release**: a per-mode tool allowlist (it re-declares a per-TOOL fact in the wrong home, must enumerate every composed tool, and rots silently as tools arrive) and an `access` sandbox cap (it fused the collaboration stance with an enforcement level — flipping the sandbox option while planning silently did nothing) (L166, L168). The exit review runs through the **user-interaction** seam, not the approval seam, because the approval seam's outcome vocabulary is closed and one-shot, so a rejection carries no feedback and an approval can never grow options (L180). A mode's payload carries **no reason/provenance field** — the cause is log-adjacent (a tool-driven flip sits next to its `tool/call`; a user flip sits at its turn boundary) (L79). `exit_plan_mode` is registered once and never filtered, so native tool schemas and the Code Mode SDK stay byte-identical across mode switches.
- Edge cases / failure handling: `mode/set` is not a surface node, so **compaction cannot shadow it**; `foldMode` folds the append-only log directly (L94). A folded mode name the current config no longer defines behaves as the default **without a notice** — a resumed log answers to the config it finds (L136). `execute` rejects an agent-less call, rejects any folded mode other than `plan`, rejects an empty or heading-less plan before asking the reviewer, and only exactly one `Approve` selection consents — every other shape fails closed (L106). With no user-interaction provider, `ask()` throws `NO_PROVIDER`, the tool returns the corrective `isError`, and the exit degrades to the user toggling modes — never to an unreviewed exit (L138). A pending user flip set while idle is **lost if the process dies before the next turn** (accepted cost, with the UI re-applying it) (L197).
- Surprising: The accepted cost is stated plainly: "**a mode restrains by guidance alone** — a model that ignores the section CAN mutate during plan; the review moment, the session log, and independent sandbox, approval, and filesystem policies are the containment surface" (L197). Narration happens only when the model-visible state actually changed, so a net-zero flip sequence (plan then back, all before the boundary) narrates nothing (L134). A mode transition changes the system prompt at order 50, so the cache path changes from that point on — but the tool schemas and Code Mode SDK no longer churn (L197). The note surveys eight shipped plan modes and finds the same five parts everywhere (L150).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-06-sandbox.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-07-06-sandbox.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the subprocess sandbox design (same 213 lines, same four-entry `cordis.yml` composition and the same FAQ).
- Key abstractions: Same `ctx.sandbox` seam, same `SandboxMode` / `SandboxEnforcement` / `SandboxExecutionPolicy` / `SandboxPolicy` vocabulary, same `sessionProjections` knob units, same `PermissionPresetService`.
- Data flow: Same per-call policy precedence (explicit escalation > session override > config default, `SessionHeader.cwd` > fallback root) and the same "the log is the store" fold for per-session modes.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the three load-bearing reasons — the escalation retry is a **new logged call** (a hidden re-entry would make one `tool/call` produce two executions with different policies), the escalation fields are capability-gated, and the stable-system-prompt sandbox statement was **shipped first and then removed on live evidence** of a soft lockout (L152, L153, L159).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same fail-closed `SANDBOX_UNAVAILABLE`, same attributable-`ENOENT`/`EACCES` runner classification with informational-line exclusion, same "denial is a result fact orthogonal to exit code" rule (L54, L80, L193).
- Surprising: Faithfully preserves the "runner attribution is an in-band protocol, so a confined child can mimic a fatal runner line — not a bypass" accepted limit and the "a granted escalation is not a working sandbox" distinction (L183, L187).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-07-session-prefix.md
- Purpose: The retired request-only prefix seam — a channel for session-stable openers (skills catalog, AGENTS.md digest, workspace baseline) that must be seen on every request but must not become permanent history.
- Key abstractions: the `agent/session-prefix` waterfall; the canonical prepend contribution `[mine, ...await next()]`; `EpochHeader.messagePrefix` as the prefix's only durable record; the `dsh-agent-loop/invariant` companion.
- Data flow: The loop fires the waterfall once per loop instance, lazily before that instance's first `agent/pre-step`; the composed list is deep-cloned, deep-frozen, cached on the instance, and placed in front of the ENTIRE derived history — directly after the provider's system slot — on every request the instance sends.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The prefix is **request-only and header-logged** — `deriveMessages()` never returns it, and its only durable record is the header snapshot, so no new session event exists and the reconstructability invariant can recompute it (L22). It is **frozen per instance**, and "reuse is structural, not disciplined": the cached product cannot change mid-session, so the provider's prompt cache holds *by construction* and the prefix extends the cacheable region at zero marginal cost per step (L23). The seam creates a **routing rule** — session-frozen openers ride the prefix; content that changes mid-session rides the append-only history channels, each a durable `context/message` paid once and prefix-cached thereafter (L23).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A composition interrupted by cancel/dispose is **discarded, never cached**, so an abort-aware listener's degraded fallback cannot leak into later requests and the next turn recomposes under a live signal (L24). A process restart or `ctx.agents.resume()` is a new instance: it recomposes and any drift lands attributably on the `'resume'` header snapshot. An empty composition is canonical absence — no extra header bytes and the request is the bare derivation (L46).
- Surprising: Compaction structurally cannot touch the prefix or the system prompt because it rewrites surface nodes and header state never enters the surface (L26). The per-step `before`/`after` slots were rejected because an `after` slot behind the growing history re-pays its tokens on every request and makes everything after it uncacheable — and the note measures that *every* current update pattern is served more cheaply by a durable append (L34).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-08-background-subagent-tasks.md
- Purpose: Adds background execution to the subagent tool so slow, independent delegations do not hold the parent call open or run serially.
- Key abstractions: the `run_in_background` parameter on each `dsh-tool-subagent` instance (gated by `enableRunInBackground`, on by default); the generic background job runtime (`ctx.jobs`); the `job_output` / `job_list` / `job_kill` tools.
- Data flow: A background call validates the parent and refuses an already-aborted signal before `ctx.jobs.start()`; the job runtime preflights the control API and owner cleanup, then a starter creates its own `AbortController` and begins `ctx.subagents.start()`; **after the id is returned the tool-call signal no longer owns the child** (L22). `done` awaits provider startup, the child result and `run.dispose()`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Background subagents reuse the **generic** job runtime rather than getting subagent-specific wait/output/stop tools, because capability-specific tools would duplicate the task protocol and teach a second collect-and-stop habit (L18, L45). The owner fence applies to subagents like every other producer even when agents and logs are session-scoped, because the job registry and predictable ids are runtime-global (L53). The tool exposes only the child's **final** output — streaming child history into the parent would blur the log boundary and make provider behaviour diverge (L57).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Startup, result and disposal failures become **failed outcomes rather than rejected task promises** (L28). `readOutput` is absent: while the task is live `job_output` returns status only, and after settlement it returns the final output idempotently (L29). A background child belongs to its parent and is **not durable across owner closure** — agent disposal cancels the task and awaits startup rollback or child disposal before `AgentHandle.dispose()` resolves (L33). A completion notice whose injection target was already disposed is dropped: "cleanup, not notification, is the lifecycle guarantee" (L35).
- Surprising: The runtime boundary is enforced by authorisation and owner cleanup *independently of prompt compliance* — the model guidance (retain ids, keep working instead of busy-polling, collect before answering, kill irrelevant work) is advisory only (L39).

- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "generic runtime instead of subagent-specific companion tools" reasoning and the runtime-global owner fence (L18, L53).

- Surprising: Keeps the line that cleanup — not notification — is the lifecycle guarantee (L35).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-08-repeat-tool-guard.md
- Purpose: A loop-hygiene plugin that notices a model re-issuing the same tool call with byte-identical arguments and injects an advisory reminder so it changes course.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-repeat-tool-guard` in the new `guard/` package group; a `WeakMap` keyed by the live `Agent` object; the chain key `(tool name, canonical arguments)`; the `thresholds` / `include` / `exclude` / `argumentsPreviewChars` config.
- Data flow: Two listeners. `tools/post-execute` is the **one** detection point: it receives `(exec, result)` together, so counting and reminder delivery need no cross-event pending map, and it also fires for **denied** calls — a model hammering a denied call is exactly the loop worth breaking (L20). `agent/prompt-submit` is a pure reset hook: a user interjection changes the context, so repetition across it is not a loop (L21). On a threshold hit the reminder is prepended to the downstream decision's `additionalContexts`.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The reminder rides `additionalContexts` and **never** replaces the tool result's `content`, because that would make the logged `tool/result` lie about what the tool returned (L34, L58). Canonicalisation is a deep key-sort plus `JSON.stringify`, and the pi original's bigint/circular/`undefined` handling is deliberately dropped because `ToolExecution.arguments` is by construction the loop's `JSON.parse` output (L25). Two rules are documented in the package README because a reader would otherwise guess: **untracked calls are transparent to the chain** (an excluded call neither increments nor resets, so `grep X → todo_write → grep X` still counts as two consecutive `grep X` — bookkeeping tools must not launder a loop), and calls with no agent are ignored (L29, L30). State is keyed per agent because the tool registry is a context-level singleton whose waterfalls interleave every agent's calls (L18).
- Edge cases / failure handling: `thresholds` is validated at load and throws on an empty list, a non-integer, a value below 2 or a duplicate — misconfiguration fails loud instead of silently falling back to defaults (L48). `include`/`exclude` entries are **predicates over the tools that exist at call time**, not references to registry entries, so an entry matching no registered tool is *not* an error — deliberately unlike `toolOrder`'s referent check, so `exclude: [mcp_*]` stays valid in a deployment with no MCP tools (L48). The detailed reminder's argument preview is head-truncated at `argumentsPreviewChars` (default 500) so a looping `write`-sized payload cannot ride into the next request unbounded, while the chain key still compares the full canonical string (L34).
- Surprising: Escalating to `block` at the highest threshold was rejected for the initial scope because a blocked call punishes legitimate identical repeats (polling a long-running terminal, re-checking a file the agent expects to change) (L60). The plugin fixed a bug in the pi original, which hardcoded the gentle text to the literal count 3 while the guard keys it to `thresholds[0]` (L34).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-08-self-referential-cordis-toolset.md
- Purpose: Runtime API discovery and runner isolation — letting a model inspect the APIs a plugin can actually call, and running process-local definitions in a confined realm.
- Key abstractions: `cordis_inspect_list` and `cordis_inspect_query` (read-only runtime discovery); the generated catalog intersected with live providers; the Host runner's vm realm plus a context façade.
- Data flow: A Host definition evaluates in a fresh vm realm and receives a façade where service access requires declared injection, framework internals are hidden and registrations belong to the definition's fiber; tool output normalisation crosses back into the Host realm before validation; disposal awaits the fiber's owned effects.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The generated catalog **retains source-owned declarations and JSDoc** and is intersected with live providers, and the catalog generator **rejects freshness drift** — hand-maintained API tables were rejected because they drift independently from the service declarations (L14, L22). The vm is explicitly **not a security boundary**: restricted globals improve lifecycle correctness, not permission enforcement, because the injected filesystem, shell and network services still have real authority (L16, L24). Detailed queries exist so that complete API declarations are not charged to every request (L14).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Definitions remain **process-local** — restart and session resume do not recreate them from historical calls, because replaying source would execute historical side effects; historical cards display persisted source and outcomes without restoring a live definition (L18, L26). Shipped model tools do not create or mutate runner definitions.
- Surprising: The note distinguishes a *discovery* problem from a *permission* problem and refuses to conflate them — a façade can hide framework internals while still handing out real authority (L10, L24).

- Surprising: Keeps the note's honest note about the browser consumers: removing their write-side registry needs a coordinated replacement, and session data must not be assumed to recreate live state (L30).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-09-bash-backed-grep-glob-discovery.md
- Purpose: Adds model-facing `glob` and `grep` as bash-backed conditional tools, with a two-layer budget and a tool-owned formatted-result spill.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-tool-fs-search`; the `glob(pattern, path?)` and `grep(pattern, path?, include?)` schemas; the config budgets `globMaxResults` 100, `grepMaxMatches` 250, `grepMaxLineBytes` 2000, `rawOutputMaxBytes` 20 MB, `timeoutMs` 30 s; the package-private `shell-quote.ts` and `search-core.ts` helpers; the `SEARCH_*` error vocabulary.
- Data flow: At load the package probes `command -v rg` through `ctx.bash.resolve(request)` then `ctx.bash.run(spec)`; execution uses the same flow with fixed `rg` templates (`rg --files --glob … --sort=modified --no-ignore --hidden` for glob, `rg --json` for grep), forwarding `exec.signal` and requesting `stdoutMaxBytes: rawOutputMaxBytes` (L16, L79, L81). The tool parses only untruncated stdout within the cap; over-cap or still-truncated output is a clear search failure telling the model to narrow `pattern`, `path` or `include` (L83).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The tools are **not** `ctx.fs` provider methods, because search is product behaviour rather than a universal text-storage primitive and a remote backend may have no shared ripgrep view (L10, L126). Registration is conditional on the probe: "a model-visible tool schema is a promise that the deployment can attempt that capability", so a missing `rg` means *no* tools and no prompt guidance — but a probe that cannot start, times out, aborts or yields no exit code **fails plugin load loudly**, because that is a broken bash executor rather than an absent optional binary (L16, L144). The tools own their formatted-result spill because the generic post-execute spill policy sees only the final result and cannot recover matches the tool already omitted (L93, L136). Two layers of budget are kept: a 20 MB raw internal buffer and small inline caps.
- Edge cases / failure handling: ripgrep exit semantics are tool-owned: exit 0 is success with matches, exit 1 is success with **no** matches, while an invalid pattern, a runtime `rg` disappearance and an inaccessible workdir are failures (L87). An `aborted` bash run returns a structured failure rather than pretending there were no matches (L87). Search failures use a package-owned `HarnessError` subclass with `SEARCH_INVALID_PATTERN` / `SEARCH_FAILED` / `SEARCH_RAW_OUTPUT_OVERFLOW` / `SEARCH_ABORTED` — **not** `FsErrorCode`, because these are not `ctx.fs` provider operations (L89). "Search success must not turn into an `isError` result solely because formatted-result spill storage is unavailable" (L97). Only stdout is a parse source; stderr is diagnostic and a truncated stderr uses the retained tail with a truncation note.
- Surprising: `include` is one positive glob filter — comma-separated or negated include patterns are rejected up front with a structured argument error, and **every** model-controlled value in the shell command must pass the package-private quoting helper (L75). The spill artifact stores the **complete** formatted match list, not only the omitted tail, so the retrieval hint points at the same logical result the model saw (L81).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-10-agent-session-identity-and-log-location.md
- Purpose: Lets a model identify the session that owns a bash call, through a managed environment registry rather than ambient process state.
- Key abstractions: the `ctx.shellEnv` registry (contributors declare a name, the `DSH_*` keys they may return, a description per key and `resolve(execution)`); `DSH_ENV_PREFIX` as the single namespace source with `DshEnvironmentKey` derived from its `typeof`; `ShellExecRequest.dshEnv` / `ShellExecSpec.dshEnv` as a typed managed overlay separate from the ordinary `env`.
- Data flow: The registry rebuilds a trusted overlay for every foreground and background bash `ToolExecution`: `DSH_HOME` (absolute configured home), `DSH_SHELL=1` (always), and `DSH_SESSION_ID` (equal to `agent.session.header.id` when the execution has an agent). The local executor removes every inherited ambient managed key, applies its ordinary scrub / terminal environment / explicit `env`, and **finally** merges the trusted `dshEnv` snapshot (L28).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Two properties are stated up front: the owner of a fact decides how to resolve it, and every child receives a per-execution snapshot rather than process-global mutable state — so a nested harness cannot leak its ambient `DSH_*` values into a child whose agent, backend or configuration differs (L12). Because the trusted overlay is merged **last**, an ordinary caller's `env` entry can never displace a managed value, which guarantees "a missing value means absent now rather than inherited from an outer or previous harness" (L28). The registry detects key conflicts at registration and stays enumerable, whereas a typed waterfall event cannot declare ownership without running and a later listener can silently overwrite keys (L56). The peer-product rule is distilled as: inject identity at the invocation boundary, let storage resolve location, and never use a process-global current-session variable in a concurrent harness (L36).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Duplicate contributor names, duplicate key ownership, reserved keys, malformed declarations, undeclared runtime output and non-string output all fail loudly; registration is a Cordis effect removed with the contributing fiber (L16). A transcript-location fact was deliberately dropped — the earlier `DSH_SESSION_JSONL` / `transcript_path` design was removed because those paths were only readable with compression disabled (L24, L32). A fresh session receives its id before the first turn, so its first bash call can already read `DSH_SESSION_ID`; resume reuses the loaded header while fork and spawn create new ids (L40). If top-level `dshHome` and `skills.local.dshHome` resolve differently, composition fails rather than exposing contradictory homes (L42).
- Surprising: The namespace is **discoverable but not secret** — `DSH_HOME` can reveal a configured root and a command can override variables in its own shell syntax, so consumers must rely on sandbox/filesystem policy rather than variable secrecy for authorisation (L65).

- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the two boundary properties (owner resolves the fact; every child gets a per-execution snapshot) and the "missing means absent now" guarantee (L12, L28).

- Surprising: Keeps the peer-product portable rule and the "namespace is discoverable, not secret" honesty (L36, L65).
### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-10-session-query-service.md
- Purpose: One service for exact inspection of session history across the live store and the optional persistence backend, without every consumer re-implementing source precedence.
- Key abstractions: the abstract `ctx.sessionQuery` service over one logical corpus; `listSessions()` / `filterSessions(filters)` / `listEvents(sessionId)` / `filterEvents(sessionId, filters)` / `readEvent(request)` / `traceSession` / `traceEvent`; `foldSurface(events)` shared with `SurfaceManager`.
- Data flow: Cross-corpus listing asks the active backend for authoritative metadata and then overlays a fresh live-store list; matching ids become one `SessionRecord` where the live header wins and `live`/`persisted` report source availability independently. An exact target read checks the live store first and snapshots the live header and event log; with no live target it lists persistence metadata, proves the id exists, loads it and rejects a list/load header mismatch. All returned headers and events cross one structured-clone boundary.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The live path **never consults persistence**, so a failing durable backend cannot make known live history unreadable (L20). There is **no persisted cache and no invalidation listener** — exact reads can ask the authoritative sources directly, and caching would add lifecycle and concurrency state before scale requires it (L36). `readEvent()` returns a cloned `SessionHeader`, not a source-availability record, because determining a live target's persisted flag would break that guarantee (L26). `listEvents()` and `traceEvent()` classify raw events using the same `foldSurface()` the model-history derivation uses, so inspection cannot disagree with derivation about positional replacement semantics (L24).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Immutable header disagreement is `SESSION_QUERY_SOURCE_CONFLICT` (L18). `before`/`after` neighbours default to zero and are independently bounded by `readWindowMax` (default 50). The service is context-wide **trusted infrastructure, not an authorization layer** — a future model-facing history tool or human UI must apply explicit caller/session scope (L30).
- Surprising: Full-text search was deliberately kept **out** of the exact-read service, because putting provider coordination, synchronisation, invalidation, ranking and cursor state there would create a second state machine beside the concrete database owner (L12). The inherited implementation has exactly one source-resolution state variable — the currently mounted persistence service — with no provider queues, fingerprints, extractor registries or derived-index updates (L41).

- Data flow: Same live-first exact read that never touches persistence, and the same live-header-wins merge for cross-corpus listings (L18, L20).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "no persisted cache, no invalidation listener" reasoning and the separation of full-text search from the exact-read service (L12, L36).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same `SESSION_QUERY_SOURCE_CONFLICT` and the same cloned-header-not-availability-record rule (L18, L26).
- Surprising: Keeps the note's own "trusted infrastructure, not an authorization layer" boundary statement (L30).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-10-sqlite-session-query-provider.md
- Purpose: Adds full-text session search over SQLite FTS5, in a separate derived database, with a live TEMP overlay for the current session.
- Key abstractions: `searchSessions(request, exec?)` / `searchEvents(request, exec?)` returning cursor-paginated hits with bounded plain-text snippets but **no provider identifier and no numeric relevance score**; the branded `SessionSearchCursor`; `SessionResultFilter` / `SessionEventResultFilter`; the `unicode61` tokenizer.
- Data flow: One serialised operation reads the provider-neutral persistence snapshot listing, compares each source-qualified opaque revision with the revision stored beside the indexed session, loads only new or changed logs, reconciles rows in one transaction and then executes the query; the caller's exact abort signal is passed into listing and inspection, each observer operation is awaited, and cancellation is checked after each await (L38). Live sessions use connection-local TEMP tables that shadow the persisted base for the same id and reveal it on detach.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The derived schema gets **its own application id and monotonic schema version**, and a database with a foreign application id or unrecognised user tables is **refused before journal-mode mutation** — this prevents an accidentally configured canonical session database from being changed (L42). The index lives in a **separate** database because a rebuildable index must not share the authoritative log's schema, reset or failure boundary (L48). Queries are trimmed, whitespace-normalised and quoted as **one literal FTS5 phrase** with embedded quotes doubled, so `OR`, `NEAR`, quotes, parentheses and `*` remain data rather than executable query syntax; NUL is rejected before SQLite execution (L28). Ordering is deterministic *and comparable across the persistent and TEMP tables* — actual FTS5 highlighted-match span count descending, then document code-point length, event time, session id, seq — because BM25 scores from differently populated corpora are not comparable (L26, L52).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A source or extraction failure cannot mark a row current, and a transaction failure rolls back so a later search retries (L38). Conflicting immutable live and durable headers **fail** rather than combining sources. On POSIX, missing directories and database files are created owner-only so new SQLite sidecars inherit that mode; existing modes are preserved. One service in one process exclusively owns a derived-index path — cross-process writers are unsupported because generations and live TEMP shadow state are connection-owned (L42). Cancellation is prompt while queued; once source observation starts it settles that query observer without committing, and a shared cold load already admitted for another observer may continue (L38).
- Surprising: The `unicode61` tokenizer was chosen after an experiment — it supports the two-character token `AI` and produces an index about 2.1× smaller than the trigram alternative — and the accepted limitation is stated plainly: `AI` does not match the larger token `BRAID`, and arbitrary substring search uses the provider-independent text scan instead (L32). Node's synchronous `DatabaseSync` MATCH calls cannot be interrupted once running on the JS thread, so the service brackets them with signal checks but does not promise mid-statement preemption (L44). The service never calls the backend's mutating `load()` for an id currently owned by `ctx.sessions` (L38).

- Data flow: Same single serialised observe → reconcile → query operation with cancellation checks around each await (L38).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the separate-derived-database rule and the "foreign application id refused before journal-mode mutation" safety (L42, L48), plus the literal-phrase quoting that keeps MATCH operators as data (L28).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same rollback-on-transaction-failure rule, same conflicting-header failure, same owner-only POSIX creation and single-process ownership (L38, L42).
- Surprising: Faithfully keeps the measured tokenizer trade (2.1× smaller index, `AI` does not match `BRAID`) and the non-preemptible synchronous SQLite section (L32, L44).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-13-session-query-tracing.md
- Purpose: Adds lineage and event-relationship tracing to the session-query service, keeping positional replacement and cited-source relationships as distinct concepts.
- Key abstractions: `traceSession(sessionId)` → `SessionLineageTrace`; `traceEvent({sessionId, seq})` → `SessionEventTrace` with `replacedBy`, `replacementChain`, `replacedEventSeqs`, `sourceEventSeqs`, `derivedEventSeqs`; the one-pass surface fold.
- Data flow: Both traces are **one-shot views** over the existing live-preferred corpus — session tracing consumes one complete corpus listing, event tracing consumes one loaded logical log plus one canonical surface fold; the service retains no lineage, reverse-index or replacement state after the call (L14).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The two relationship kinds are kept **separate**: a positional replacement can shadow surface nodes while also citing non-surface construction inputs, and consumers need to distinguish those meanings (L18, L29). The trace does **not** follow cited source events transitively, because that would obscure logged direct evidence, grow result size and let one malformed distant edge alter otherwise local output (L30). Lineage ordering is deterministic: parents immediate-to-outward, siblings by creation time then session id (L16).
- Edge cases / failure handling: The one-pass surface fold accepts or rejects the loaded log **as a whole** — zero-based contiguous seqs, surface-marker eligibility, only surface event types may cite source seqs, non-empty duplicate-free arrays, every source is an earlier seq, and every positional replacement names and cites all the surface nodes it removes; every failure is `SESSION_QUERY_INVALID_SURFACE` with no weaker classification-only standard (L22). A cycle connected to the target fails with `SESSION_QUERY_INVALID_LINEAGE`; `complete: false` carries the first unresolved parent id (L16). A known live event trace never consults persistence.
- Surprising: Best-effort traces over a malformed source-event list were rejected because "a structurally plausible partial result would look authoritative" — exact inspection fails loudly when the canonical relationship contract is broken (L31).

- Data flow: Same one-shot, stateless view over the live-preferred corpus (L14).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "different meanings, different edges" argument and the non-transitive cited-source rule (L29, L30).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same `SESSION_QUERY_INVALID_SURFACE` and `SESSION_QUERY_INVALID_LINEAGE` failures with no partial results (L16, L22).
- Surprising: Keeps the "structurally plausible partial result would look authoritative" reason for failing loudly (L31).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-14-cross-family-fs-sandbox.md
- Purpose: Extends the sandbox mode vocabulary to the in-process filesystem tools, with one shared policy home and the same escalation lever bash already had.
- Key abstractions: `ctx.sandboxPolicy` (`@deepseek-ai/dsh-sandbox-policy`) as the single owner of `mode` + `workspaceRoot` + the `sandbox/mode` event and the `sandboxMode` projection unit; `dsh-fs-sandbox` registering `SandboxedFileSystem extends LocalFileSystem` as `ctx.fs`; `writableRoots(policy)`; `WIDER_MODES`; `approveEscalation` taking a minimal structural `EscalationApprover`.
- Data flow: `ctx.sandboxPolicy.resolve({session?, mode?})` returns a complete per-call `SandboxExecutionPolicy` (explicit approved mode > session projected override > `defaultMode`; session cwd > configured root). The fs provider passes reads through untouched and enforces the two mutations before delegating to the inherited atomic write; `read-only` denies outright, `workspace-write` fences the canonicalised target against the writable-root set, `danger-full-access` delegates unfenced (L33–37). The tool layer maps `FS_SANDBOX_DENIED` to the same `[sandbox: file access denied under <mode> mode]` marker and resolves the same `ctx.approval` request (L45).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Enforcement happens **in the provider, not on an intent event**, for two mechanical reasons: the `fs/*` intent slots are single-decision first-wins and already occupied by `dsh-fs-observation-policy`, and the intent events are dispatched only by `dsh-tool-fs` — a direct `ctx.fs` caller bypasses them, while provider-level enforcement covers every caller by construction (L53, L64). The gap was *not* read-only-shaped: a deny-all fs fence would be strictly worse than disabling the fs tools, because the model would try an in-workspace write, be denied, and learn to detour through bash heredocs — so cross-family enforcement must speak the full ladder including path-containment judgement (L12). The fs fence is a **policy boundary, not a kernel one**: the operations are the seam's own and only the target path is untrusted, so canonicalise-then-contain is the complete answer to this surface; kernel isolation of untrusted code stays `ctx.shell`'s job (L41, L90). `approveEscalation` takes a *structural* approver generic over agent and call-id types so `dsh-sandbox` gains no dependency on the approval or agent packages (L47).
- Edge cases / failure handling: `FS_SANDBOX_DENIED` is distinct from `FS_PERMISSION_DENIED` — "a host EACCES is the world refusing; this is policy refusing" — and there is no text inference (L39). Canonical spellings take a lexical containment fast path, but where Windows exposes one directory through different casing or 8.3 spellings an ancestor walk compares **filesystem identity** rather than weakening the boundary to textual prefix guesses (L36). The target is re-canonicalised immediately before delegating, so an ancestor symlink swapped since the tool resolved it is caught (L36). The residual resolve-to-syscall TOCTOU is narrowed, not eliminated. A direct `ctx.fs.writeText` with no per-call stamp is confined at the deployment default (L84). The system prompt still states no sandbox mode — the marker teaches the boundary at the moment it matters (L49).
- Surprising: Fence-vs-runner parity is **derived, not asserted** — the fs fence and the Seatbelt profile both take their writable set from `writableRoots`, and a parity unit test pins the sets, so a runner profile changing its writable set without that function would drift (L92). Per-family policy config with a load-time consistency check was rejected because "the policy service makes drift inexpressible instead of detected" (L69).

- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "enforce in the provider, not the intent gate" reasoning with its two mechanical facts, and the "policy boundary, not a kernel boundary" threat model (L41, L53).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same `FS_SANDBOX_DENIED` vs `FS_PERMISSION_DENIED` distinction, same filesystem-identity ancestor walk for Windows aliases, same re-canonicalisation before delegating (L36, L39).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-14-time-context-plugin.md
- Purpose: Gives the model a live clock and an inter-turn duration without a tool call, derived per step from durable session timestamps.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-time-context` (an opt-in function plugin in the `context/` group, which holds bounded request-context enrichments that define neither a tool nor a service); the global `context:time` prompt section at order 10; `refreshIntervalMs` (default 60,000); `timeZone` (IANA identifier).
- Data flow: At a turn's first assembly the provider scans before `turn/start` for the latest `user/message`, `assistant/message`, `tool/result`, `context/message` or `steering/message` — excluding the current prompt so the duration expresses the inter-turn gap rather than approximately zero — and the section renders an ISO-shaped timestamp with numeric UTC offset and IANA zone plus a compact whole-second duration. Every refresh within the turn keeps the same baseline; the first turn reports `unavailable (no earlier message in this session)` (L24).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The baseline is the **session event's append time**, not an unlogged client timestamp, so resume and fork are deterministic from the durable log and the model-visible value stays reconstructable without a new event (L26). One section records the timestamp and the elapsed pair **atomically** — separate `{{current_time}}` and `{{elapsed}}` variables were rejected because independent providers can sample different instants and would need shared caching (L47). Refresh is **request-bound**: no timer creates work during model calls, tools or idle time, because a new value has no consumer outside request assembly (L30, L48). The section is empty for bare and idle assemblies.
- Edge cases / failure handling: `refreshIntervalMs` must be a non-negative safe integer; an explicit `timeZone` must be an IANA identifier, validated at load. An omitted `timeZone` resolves the Node process zone once at plugin load (`TZ` → host/container), so the captured zone is stable until reload and the ISO-shaped local timestamp carries its current numeric offset so daylight-saving changes stay explicit (L32). A backward wall-clock adjustment clamps the duration to zero (L26). The loop records the block in full `request/header` snapshots before transmission, satisfying the reconstructable-requests contract, and each request carries one current block — earlier readings do not remain in conversation history (L36).
- Surprising: The duration reflects **harness processing time at durable append boundaries**, not client-network latency before logging; preserving a client-origin timestamp would need a separate durable input contract (L60). The note is already superseded on the storage/refresh contract by "Durable per-step time context" while the opt-in package, zoned formatting and validation survive (L10).

- Surprising: Keeps the honest note that duration excludes client-network latency and needs a separate durable input contract to preserve (L60).
### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-16-harness-level-loop.md
- Purpose: Introduces goal-based execution *above* the turn/step loop, as two explicit plugin policies rather than one universal "loop" service.
- Key abstractions: the same-session hierarchy **Goal → Goal Round → Turn → Step** and the fresh-agent hierarchy **Ralph Run → Ralph Round → fresh child Turn → Step**; `dsh-goal` (domain: `GoalId`, compare-and-set `GoalRef`, `GoalSnapshot`, four-state `GoalPhase`, `GoalBlockReason`, process-local `GoalActivation`); `dsh-tool-goal`; `dsh-goal-round-driver`; `dsh-commands`; `dsh-command-goal`; `dsh-tool-ralph`.
- Data flow: Every goal mutation commits through a durable `goal/change` event carrying a full versioned snapshot or a revisioned clear tombstone; the session log is the only durable source of truth, so persistence, resume and `SessionStore.fork()` carry the goal with no second database (L50). The round driver reserves, fences, admits, attributes, settles, cancels and drains goal rounds; only an admitted positive-round goal-sourced `user/message` charges a round.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Durable phase and live activation are **separate**: durable phases are `active`/`paused`/`blocked`/`complete`, while activation is `armed`/`disarmed` and is **never persisted** — "reopening a session never starts goal work by itself", because opening a session is observation, not authority to spend resources (L52, L107). A blocked goal carries a required `GoalBlockReason` with a stable lower-kebab-case `code`; usage limits, round exhaustion, model failures and policy rejection are **reason codes, not extra lifecycle phases** (L52). The driver's `agent/pre-step` fence checks the goal facts both before and after downstream listeners, so an edit, pause, human message or unload race cannot admit obsolete work (L62). The model-facing tool set is only `get_goal`/`create_goal`/`update_goal`, and mutating calls require a direct human message in a live root-agent turn (L72).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A stale reservation closes a blocked no-step turn **without consuming the cap**, and a concurrent goal revision wins over settlement from an older round (L64). Cancellation pauses; rate limiting blocks with `usage-limited`; cap exhaustion blocks with `round-limit`; queue failure uses `queue-failed` (L66). The goal driver never invents another round after an abnormal terminal outcome. A fork inherits the durable goal prefix but starts **disarmed**, so inheritance does not imply execution authority and no synthetic cancellation is inserted into history (L56).
- Surprising: The note explicitly refuses a universal loop service — `Evaluator`, `BudgetPolicy`, `RoundHandoff`, `GoalReflector`, background job ownership, persistence and scheduling "do not form one coherent mandatory abstraction", and building them before their first concrete consumer would duplicate existing session/workflow/subagent machinery (L102). It also refuses to copy Claude Code's transcript-only model evaluator, calling it "one useful policy, not a generally trustworthy completion certificate" (L106). The only core-loop addition is a generic observe-before-cancel `agent/cancel-requested` notification (L31).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-16-durable-per-step-time-context.md
- Purpose: The current time-context contract — a per-step, durable, request-owned reading of time and browser time zone, replacing the earlier request-only clock.
- Key abstractions: the opt-in `@deepseek-ai/dsh-time-context` plugin; an `agent/pre-step` listener that **prepends** and delegates first; the exact snapshot source `{kind:'plugin', plugin:'time-context', form:'snapshot', sections:[{name:'time-context', text}]}`; `refreshIntervalMs`.
- Data flow: When the downstream decision enters and a reading is due, the plugin combines that decision's final messages with durable user messages already in the open turn, derives browser-zone provenance from exact `user-rpc` sources, and appends one reading to the decision. The entered step appends its returned messages followed by the reading after `step/start` and before request derivation, so each reading remains a normal surface node until compaction shadows it (L18, L52).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Replacing a dynamic system-prompt value was rejected because replacement erases prior readings and changes reconstructed historical requests — a request-only clock cannot preserve the evidence behind earlier time-sensitive reasoning (L10, L58). The browser zone is **request-local** and is never copied to `SessionHeader`, a connection default or Schedule state, because travel and concurrent tabs must not mutate shared meaning (L22, L59). Provenance is re-derived from the original user-rpc messages rather than copied into a second authority (L60). One unique zone in the open turn resolves the request; multiple zones produce a sorted `mixed` result and no zone is `unavailable` — and mixed/unavailable tells the model to **ask the user to clarify** rather than guess (L20).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Rejection, listener failure or an already-aborted signal records nothing (L18). A positive interval scans raw session events for the latest plugin reading and injects when none exists, wall time moved backward, or the event is old enough — so the **event timestamp** governs after compaction and resume, with no process-local cache (L28). The baseline is the latest durable preceding user/assistant/tool-result message; a new session reports `unavailable` (L40). Backward wall-clock movement clamps to zero (L48).
- Surprising: The plugin contributes nothing to system-prompt assembly or `request/header` — request reconstruction obtains the complete durable surface prefix at each `step/start`, so historical requests recover the exact time and browser policy the model saw (L54). The Schedule tool therefore still requires an explicit `time_zone` rather than importing the prose reading, because "prose context is not a stable typed default" (L22, L61).

- Surprising: Keeps the note that the plugin adds nothing to prompt assembly or the header, so reconstruction recovers the exact reading from the durable surface (L54).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-17-dedicated-full-screen-tui-front-door.md
- Purpose: Defines `@deepseek-ai/dsh-tui` — a dedicated full-screen terminal front door that owns input and presentation only.
- Key abstractions: the TUI as a Cordis plugin over the same agent/session/tool/user-interaction services as every other front door; `presentCall`/`presentResult` tool cards; the shared `userInteraction` provider; `ctx.llm` catalog selection.
- Data flow: The host supplies the exact generated or resumed `SessionId`; the TUI waits for the matching root agent and enters full-screen mode only after that agent exists, so a matching `agent-loop/config-start-failed` event is reported **before** screen takeover (L22). The TUI rebuilds the transcript from append-origin session events, so resumed history keeps every message the reader saw and a compacted range stays readable behind one marker instead of matching the model-visible conversation (L26). Editor input calls `agent.send()` while idle and `agent.steer()` while a turn is running.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The plugin **requires both stdin and stdout to be TTYs and fails** rather than silently degrading to line-oriented behaviour, because a fallback hides deployment mistakes and changes interaction semantics (L18, L45). `displayText()` renders C0 and C1 controls other than line feeds as visible hexadecimal escapes before anything reaches the terminal, and only the TUI and pi-tui create ANSI sequences (L34). The model selector snapshots one provider/model/reasoning-effort target per step in agent-scoped prompt-assembly and request waterfalls, so `{{provider}}`/`{{model}}` interpolation and request routing cannot split when a command arrives mid-assembly — mutating `agent.options` was rejected for exactly that reason (L30, L47).
- Edge cases / failure handling: `/exit` and `/quit` share one path: cancel the active turn, wait for idle, then restore and close the terminal (L28). The question panel's controls hint lists only actions meaningful for the current option count, omitting navigation when exactly one option is shown. A selection that never reaches a request remains process-local rather than durable state (L30, L55).
- Surprising: The TUI renders an *append-origin* transcript rather than the model-visible conversation, so the human sees what actually happened rather than what the model was shown (L26). The built-in palette uses standard 16-colour ANSI foregrounds and leaves body text and backgrounds at terminal defaults, so host terminals remap the interface for light and dark themes with no TUI-specific theme setting (L36). The idle footer derives context occupancy from `tokenMeter` and shows the selected model and explicit reasoning effort; during a run, elapsed activity and the Escape interrupt hint replace that summary (L28).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-19-fresh-agent-ralph-workflow-tool.md
- Purpose: Adds `ralph({objective, maxRounds?})` — repeatedly give the same objective to a completely fresh worker, using the shared workspace as long-term memory and one bounded structured handoff as cross-round state.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-tool-ralph` as a separate Consumer package depending only on `ctx.tools`, `ctx.systemPrompt`, `ctx.workflowEngine` and `ctx.subagents`; `WorkflowStartRequest.subagentProvider` and `WorkflowStartRequest.maxTotalAgents`; the `RalphRoundReport` shape (`status: continue|complete|blocked`, `summary`, `evidence`, `nextSteps`, `blocker`).
- Data flow: One Ralph round creates exactly one child through the selected provider; spawn gives it a distinct session with no seed while preserving the parent's cwd, so the shared working tree is the durable authority (L32). The fixed prompt passes only the immutable objective, the current round and cap, a workspace-as-authority instruction and the previous structured report. `maxRounds` (default 256) is passed as `maxTotalAgents` so the fixed loop's round budget and the engine's runaway-child backstop cannot disagree (L28).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The tool is **foreground-only** and `run.dispose()` is awaited on every path so cancellation reaches the worker's bounded settlement and child quiescence before the call returns (L18). Provider lookup is **call-time** because effect-scoped provider registration can change under HMR, and the selected provider must exist, support structured output and report `inheritsParentContext: false` (L24). Report semantics are validated in the fixed script *and* again at the consumer boundary; `continue` requires next steps and no blocker, `complete` requires evidence with no next steps or blocker, `blocked` requires a concrete blocker (L34). Oversized handoffs **fail rather than being silently truncated**, because truncation can remove status evidence or next steps while still looking like an authoritative handoff (L36, L58).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A normally settled but unsuccessful child maps to `null` in the workflow language; the script detects that before report validation and returns `round-failed` with the failed round plus the last successful handoff, and the tool turns it into an error rather than misclassifying it as a malformed report or budget exhaustion (L38). Ralph adds no retry policy. `maxResultChars` separately bounds the complete successful parent-facing text including its envelope and truncation marker, without changing cross-round state (L36).
- Surprising: Completion and blocker envelopes explicitly say that a **worker reported** the outcome rather than presenting it as independent certification — the note lists an independent evaluator, evaluator-driven feedback round, completion certificate and adversarial verifier as deliberately deferred (L44, L70). The note also records that using `subagent_fork` was rejected because inherited completed turns are implicit, growing handoff state and violate the fresh-context contract (L56).

- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the call-time provider lookup with the `inheritsParentContext: false` requirement and the "fail rather than truncate an oversized handoff" rule (L24, L36).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same `round-failed` mapping with the last successful handoff and the no-retry policy (L38).
- Surprising: Keeps the worker-self-declaration honesty in the envelopes and the note that the workspace becomes authoritative cross-round memory, so workers must inspect and verify it rather than trust a narrative handoff (L64).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-19-human-goal-command.md
- Purpose: Gives a human a deterministic `/goal` command — status, creation, edit, pause, resume and clear — without spending a model turn.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-command-goal` as a command producer over `ctx.commands` and `ctx.goals`; the `CommandDefinition` registry; the domain verbs it calls.
- Data flow: The handler receives the exact target agent from command dispatch, reads or mutates that agent's goal through the domain service, and returns direct plain-text UI output; it imports neither adapter nor the concrete agent loop (L16). Successful goal mutations append the domain-owned `goal/change` event and **do not queue model context** — the command introduces no second audit record that could disagree with the domain event (L38).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Status output omits branded ids and compare-and-set revisions, because those are model/plugin coordination details rather than human controls, but it *does* include activation, because that fact changes whether work will continue (L34). Control words are ASCII-case-insensitive after trimming and are controls **only when they occupy the full suffix** — `/goal pause after verification` is a goal *objective*, not a partially parsed pause command (L30). Silently replacing an unfinished goal was rejected because it combines clear and create without atomicity or explicit destructive intent, and the generic command service has no modal confirmation API (L24, L55). The command follows the compact Codex shape with a **commit permalink** so the researched grammar stays verifiable as Codex evolves (L18).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Expected `GoalError` failures become one stable, branded-id-free `CommandResult.error`, so domain diagnostics do not leak compare-and-set internals into the UI and invalid operations never enter model history; other exceptions remain adapter-visible command failures, because treating programmer faults as ordinary domain errors would hide defects (L36). The handler performs only synchronous domain mutations, so request cancellation is decided by the registry before the mutation begins and there is no escaped asynchronous side effect to unwind (L36). Bare `/goal edit` is an error rather than an editor launch, because the portable unstructured command contract has no modal editor (L26).
- Surprising: The command shows that the goal is *restored but disarmed* after a resume, and reports both the durable phase and the live activation separately, because showing only "active" after a resume would be misleading (L12, L62). A durable paused goal cannot be resumed by the model tool but can by `/goal resume`, `/goal clear` removes the pointer while the log retains the revisioned tombstone (L28). Composition is explicit per surface: TUI mounts the stack by default, Web mounts the producer only in the `standard`/`code`/`cordis` presets, ACP deliberately omits command services, and the `sdk-minimal` tree omits the whole stack so one-shot callers do not silently become long-running goal operations (L42, L44).

- Edge cases / failure handling: Same sanitised `CommandResult.error` for expected domain failures versus adapter-visible failures for programmer faults (L36).
- Surprising: Keeps the per-surface composition matrix (TUI default, Web preset-scoped, ACP omitted, sdk-minimal omitted) and the note that the command does not certify completion or blockers (L44, L73).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-19-persisted-same-session-goal-domain.md
- Purpose: The durable domain behind same-session goals — one current objective per session, replayable from the log, with live authority kept separate from durable state.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-goal` in `packages/goal/goal/` exposing `ctx.goals`; the branded goal id; `GoalRef` as a compare-and-set pair of id and revision; `GoalSnapshot`; the four durable phases; the `goal/change` session event.
- Data flow: Every mutation appends a versioned `goal/change` event carrying a full snapshot or, for clear, a revisioned tombstone; the session log is the only durable source of truth, so persistence and fork inherit goal records with no second database or header field (L22). The replay fold derives lifecycle mutations only from `goal/change` and validates JSON shape, fresh ids, revision continuity, lifecycle transitions, counters and monotonic per-goal timestamps.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Durable phase and live activation are deliberately different facts: phases are `active`/`paused`/`blocked`/`complete` and activation is `armed`/`disarmed` and is **never part of the persisted snapshot** (L18). Creation and explicit resume arm; pause, completion, blocking and clear disarm; edits preserve activation and any blocker reason while resume and completion clear it. A blocked snapshot carries a policy-owned lower-kebab-case code and a normalised free-form message, so usage limits, round caps, execution failures and human-input dependencies **share one lifecycle state without losing their cause** (L18). `defaultMaxGoalRounds` (default 256) is a validated deployment setting materialised inside `create()` rather than exposed as another service verb (L16).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Every mutation carries the expected `GoalRef`, and a stale id or revision rejects (L30). A malformed current-format record **fails replay** rather than being ignored or repaired, and incremental replay stays positioned at the first corrupt event so later reads report the same durable fault (L24, L26). The service accepts only the exact live `Agent` object registered under its id. A cache built from any seed starts disarmed and every `agent/created` edge disarms it again, so resume, fork and driver replacement preserve the objective but never initiate work (L32). `GoalService.disarm(agent)` removes process-local authority with no session event, revision change or notification.
- Surprising: Goal rounds advance only from positive sequential admitted `user/message` source numbers for the current active revision, so ordinary session turns — human clarification, inspection, unrelated work — never consume the automatic-work budget (L24, L47). The note records honestly that plugins share one trusted process boundary: a direct session writer can counterfeit goal records, and strict replay detects the inconsistency and fails goal access at the offending record but does not isolate plugins or repair the log (L63).

- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the durable-phase vs live-activation split and the blocker-reason-in-one-lifecycle-state rule (L18).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same "malformed record fails replay rather than being repaired" rule and the first-corrupt-event positioning (L24, L26).
- Surprising: Keeps the honesty about counterfeit goal records by plugins sharing one trusted process boundary (L63).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-19-plugin-command-registration.md
- Purpose: Moves slash commands out of the TUI into a plugin-owned registry, so optional plugins can contribute human commands without editing an adapter.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-commands` in `packages/interaction/commands/`; the `CommandDefinition` (lowercase name without `/`, non-empty description, optional unstructured-input hint, abortable handler); `parseCommand(line)`; `list(agent)` / `find(agent, name)` / `execute(agent, line, signal)`.
- Data flow: Registration validates and detaches the metadata, freezes the effective definition and returns the exact Cordis effect disposer. `list(agent)` returns immutable name-sorted descriptors after scoped shadowing; `execute` parses and runs a known definition and returns a detached `success` or `error` result, with invalid syntax and unknown names returning `undefined` so the adapter owns its own error text (L22). An unscoped registration is global; a command-injected plugin mounted beneath an agent context inherits that agent's scope key, so its definition shadows a same-named global only for that exact agent (L28).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Commands run in a **human-only command plane**: the registry does not turn input into `user/message`, output does not become a session event, and neither is sent to the model implicitly — so a user-visible direct action can never unexpectedly consume tokens or let the model reinterpret an unknown command (L10, L34). The TUI registers its built-ins as agent-scoped definitions rather than switching on strings, and any submitted line beginning with `/` stays in the command plane — unknown input produces a terminal warning rather than falling through to `Agent.steer()` (L40). Every composed adapter exposes every registered command, and an incompatible plugin omits its registration in that deployment rather than encoding consumer identities in the shared domain (L20, L56). The child plugin declares its own `commands` injection because `agent.ctx` intentionally inherits the core agent-loop dependency API — adding a UI service to the loop just to enable scoped registration would invert the dependency graph (L28).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Duplicate names fail within one layer. Registration and removal emit the unfiltered, non-vetoing `commands/change` notification, and adapters recompute each live agent's effective view rather than inferring which sessions a change affects; the registry contains and logs each observer failure independently so a broken UI refresh cannot roll back another plugin's mutation (L30). The registry stops awaiting an uncooperative handler when the signal aborts, but the handler remains responsible for stopping external side effects it already started. Expected handler failures become `CommandResult.error`; thrown or malformed results remain adapter-visible command failures, deliberately separating UI output from durable domain mutation (L36).
- Surprising: `parseCommand` requires `/` at byte zero and preserves the **complete** adapter-delivered suffix as `rawInput`, including separator whitespace, so command-specific plugins own every further grammar decision (L24). A handler that changes durable behaviour calls the owning domain API, which records its own events — there is no second audit record that could disagree with the domain event (L58).

- Edge cases / failure handling: Same contained observer failures and the same adapter-visible-vs-`CommandResult.error` split (L30, L36).
- Surprising: Keeps the dependency-inversion argument for declaring `commands` in the child plugin instead of the agent loop (L28).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-19-same-session-goal-round-driver.md
- Purpose: The continuation policy that turns an active, armed goal into additional turns — without adding a goal branch to the concrete agent loop.
- Key abstractions: `@deepseek-ai/dsh-goal-round-driver`; the reservation identity `{goalId, revision, round: roundsStarted + 1}`; `GoalMessageSource`; the `agent/pre-step` entry fence; `GoalService.disarm(agent)`.
- Data flow: When an agent is idle, has no competing queued work and its goal is `active` plus `armed`, the driver checkpoints pending goal mutations, rechecks every predicate after the await, and then reserves the exact identity and the complete rendered prompt before calling `Agent.followup()`. The prompt JSON-quotes the objective so multiline or tag-like text stays an unambiguous data value inside the familiar frame (L24). The `agent/pre-step` waterfall is the entry fence: a positive goal source enters only when it exactly matches the pending identity *and* content, the live goal still has that id and revision, activation is still armed, and the round is still the next number — checked once before delegating and again after downstream listeners return (L26).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The second post-listener check exists because an async listener could edit or pause the goal while the old prompt still entered (L26). A goal mutation during a round advances the durable revision, so settlement of the older revision cannot overwrite it; the driver discards the old attempt outcome, reads the new projection, and continues only if the new revision is still active and armed — model-recorded completion, pause, block and edit are authoritative over the physical turn's later close reason (L34). The driver has **no configuration**: `maxGoalRounds` is resolved and persisted by `dsh-goal` and the blocking threshold by `dsh-tool-goal`, because repeating those tunables would create multiple owners for one policy (L20).
- Edge cases / failure handling: The settlement table maps each closed goal-owned turn to an action: durable `completed` continues; cancellation of a reserved/admitted round or an `aborted` result pauses and disarms; `error` with `RATE_LIMIT`/`QUOTA` blocks with `usage-limited`; other `error` blocks with `turn-error`; `max-tokens` blocks with `max-tokens`; a failed durability checkpoint **disarms without changing the durable phase**; `disposed`/`interrupted` disarm; an unknown plugin-added result blocks for inspection (L40–49). No abnormal outcome requests an automatic retry. If the pause mutation throws, the driver falls back to disarming rather than allowing cancelled automatic work to restart (L57). A stale reservation closes a blocked no-step turn and is **not charged** to the cap. A downstream policy rejection that is not caused by staleness blocks the goal rather than retrying around policy.
- Surprising: The plugin's listeners and quiescent close are nested in **one ordered Cordis effect**, because Cordis unloads sibling effects concurrently and separate registrations could remove the prompt fence while an async disposer was still draining (L65). The note also records that an inbox acceptance can win the microtask race just before unload, so the turn and even its first request may start and the round stays durably charged — "pretending that already-observed admission never happened would corrupt replay accounting" (L67).

- Edge cases / failure handling: Same settlement table, same failed-checkpoint-disarms-without-phase-change rule, same pause-throw fallback to disarm (L40–49, L57).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-20-dsh-cli-personal-config.md
- Purpose: Ships an installable `dsh` CLI and a machine-level personal-config layer rooted at the Harness home, so a developer's own provider/model/credentials live outside the checkout.
- Key abstractions: `apps/cli` (npm `@deepseek-ai/dsh`) as the product-assembly tier over `packages/*`; `dsh-app-boot` as the personal overlay loader; `resolveDshHome` from `@deepseek-ai/dsh-home-paths`; the personal `.env` and `config.yaml` (a top-level YAML array of `@cordisjs/plugin-include` `PatchOptions`).
- Data flow: One bin dispatches the default interactive TUI, `-p`/`--prompt` headless turns and the `web` surface. The personal `.env` is loaded *after* the invoking directory's `.env` and `process.loadEnvFile` never overrides, so precedence is ambient > project `.env` > personal `.env` (L22). `config.yaml` is parsed with the include's own `!!js` dialect and passed to `boot()` as the root include's `patches`; an id-targeted patch replaces the named entry's whole `config`, `insert` appends entries, and an unmatched id is a silent no-op (L23).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The personal file is a **cordis overlay over the shipped default config**, not a second config vocabulary — a pi-style typed settings file was rejected as a product-owner decision, and a personal full `cordis.yml` was rejected because it would have to name the leaf config's path, which varies per checkout; patches invert the dependency so the bin keeps choosing the tree (L34, L36). Deep-merging was rejected because it would fork the patch semantics from the committed overlays (L38). Opt-in via an env flag was rejected: personal config that is off by default never gets used — presence plus explicit per-test isolation gives live runs the overlay and tests hermeticity (L40).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A missing file means no overlay, but a present-but-unreadable, unparsable or non-array file **throws at boot** — misconfiguration fails loud, never a silent skip (L24). On HMR, every add/change/removal transactionally recomposes the full patch list through the launcher's own composition closure so the fresh patches land in the same layer position; invalid YAML or a rejected Loader candidate leaves the last good tree active and broadcasts `hmr/config-update-failed(filename, Error)` (L28).
- Surprising: The PTY smoke's launcher isolates `$DSH_HOME` to a per-test directory, exactly as it already isolates `DSH_AGENTS_HOME`, so a developer's real personal overlay cannot leak into fixtures (L26). The note records the drift risk of whole-config replacement — a personal override restates the base fields it keeps and can drift when the base entry changes shape, with the loader's warnings and `dsh --dump-config` as the diagnostics (L45).

- Surprising: Keeps the per-test `$DSH_HOME` isolation and the whole-config-replacement drift warning (L26, L45).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-20-tui-startup-slogans.md
- Purpose: Replaces a hardcoded instructional TUI welcome line with a per-boot startup slogan, then records that the whole slogan half was later replaced again.
- Key abstractions: the exported `STARTUP_SLOGANS` bank; `pickStartupSlogan` with an injectable random source; the typewriter reveal (one character per 40 ms frame with a `▌` block cursor trailing).
- Data flow: The demo leaf no longer configures `welcome`; when `welcome` is unset, `dsh-tui` picks one bank member per boot and reveals it. The reveal starts only after `ui.start()` succeeds and its interval is cleared on dispose alongside the other listeners (L17).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The slogan bank is **presentation copy, deliberately not config** — deployments that want controlled wording already have `welcome`, and the no-hardcoded-tunables rule targets deployment-varying behaviour, not brand copy (L18, L28). A fixed cooler slogan was rejected because one string re-read on every boot decays into wallpaper, exactly like the line it replaces (L26). The animation lives beside the other `createTuiChat` effects rather than inside `HeaderComponent`, which would have needed its own TUI handle and lifecycle (L30).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Boot output is no longer byte-deterministic when `welcome` is unset, so every recorded or snapshot surface pins `welcome` explicitly; the PTY smoke anchors on the reveal cursor and the session-id line instead (L34). Dispose stops a mid-reveal animation.
- Surprising: The note is **self-superseding**: the slogan bank and typewriter reveal "shipped, read as weird in use, and were replaced by a subtitle-free banner" — the archived note keeps the measurement rather than pretending the idea stuck (L8). The same change also recovered 100% per-file coverage in `packages/ui/tui/src/index.ts` by removing a dead editor border-colour reassignment and turning the colour-scheme query's `.then`/`.catch` arrows into named, tested handlers (L22).

- Data flow: Same post-`ui.start()` start and dispose-cleared interval (L17).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "brand copy is not config" rule and the fixed-slogan decay argument (L18, L26).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same non-determinism mitigation by pinning `welcome` in every recorded surface (L34).
- Surprising: Keeps the note's own admission that the shipped animation read as weird and was replaced (L8).
### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-20-windows-tui-support.md
- Purpose: Supports the full-screen TUI on Windows and, more importantly, makes the *real-process smoke* run there too.
- Key abstractions: pi-tui's `ProcessTerminal` (which already contains a native Windows console path); a platform-selected pseudo-terminal boundary — the Python POSIX PTY driver on macOS/Linux and `node-pty`/ConPTY on Windows.
- Data flow: Both drivers receive the same launch command, environment, terminal dimensions, marker-gated input actions, timeout, expected exit code and output assertions, so all three smoke scenarios run on every supported platform (L18).
- Non-obvious design decisions: The TUI platform contract follows the runtime shipped to users rather than the portability of one test driver — "a platform exclusion is justified only when the product has an unsupported runtime dependency or a demonstrated semantic gap" (L12). On Windows the product enables virtual-terminal input after raw mode and avoids the Unix-only `SIGWINCH` refresh, and the harness adds no platform rejection or reduced Windows mode (L16). Declaring the TUI unsupported on Windows was rejected because the pinned terminal runtime implements Windows console input explicitly (L24).
- Edge cases / failure handling: `node-pty` is a **test-only** dependency of the examples workspace, and its reviewed native install script is explicitly enabled in `pnpm-workspace.yaml`; production TUI packages acquire no new dependency or subprocess layer (L20). Changing that dependency or its allowed install script requires native-boundary review.
- Surprising: Running the POSIX driver through MSYS/Cygwin/WSL was rejected because it would test a compatibility environment rather than the native Windows console path users actually run (L25), and relying on renderer unit tests plus semantic terminal snapshots was rejected because fake terminals do not prove Loader boot, real raw input, process exit or terminal restoration at the OS boundary (L27).

- Key abstractions: Same `ProcessTerminal` runtime, same platform-selected PTY driver boundary.
- Data flow: Same shared scenario contract across the POSIX and ConPTY drivers (L18).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "contract follows the shipped runtime, not the test driver" rule (L12).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same test-only `node-pty` dependency with an explicitly enabled install script (L20).
- Surprising: Keeps the MSYS/Cygwin/WSL rejection and the "fake terminals prove nothing about restoration" argument (L25, L27).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-21-continuable-background-subagents.md
- Purpose: Lets a parent keep talking to a subagent **after** its background run settled, by separating the durable child session from the per-activation run.
- Key abstractions: the durable child session plus a series of Task-backed activations; `SubagentRun.steer?()` (the confirmed live-only capability); the injected internal continuation manager owning child ids, descriptor persistence and activation lifecycle; `SubagentProvider.resume?()` as the continuation capability; the versioned `SUBAGENT_DESCRIPTOR_VERSION` descriptor; `snapshotSubagentDescriptor()`.
- Data flow: Each initial or resumed activation creates a fresh Task, `AgentHandle` and `SubagentRun`, drives one turn, collects its result, and disposes the run before the Task becomes terminal (L18). The continuation manager keeps a process-local association from child session id to its current Task and, after provider publication, its run; it installs the association **before awaiting provider start or resume** and removes it only after run disposal and Task terminal publication (L49). The `send_message(agent_id, message)` tool routes to a running Task's `run.steer(message, source)` (returning the existing Job id) or, with no running Task, creates a fresh Task and cold-resumes the durable session (L63, L64).
- Non-obvious design decisions: A Task, a run and a child session have **different lifetimes** — a Task is one background turn with one terminal result, a `SubagentRun` owns one activation, and a persisted child session may contain many turns — so continuation must preserve per-run disposal rather than retain every historical child Agent in memory (L14, L95). The provider's `resume?()` **presence is the capability**, so no redundant capability flag exists (L81). The descriptor deliberately omits the merge-extensible `AgentOptions` object and `subagentDepth`: unrelated extension values must not make continuation fail merely because they are not JSON, and cold resume relies on the persisted header's `delegationDepth` (L77). The descriptor is written as one model-hidden `subagent/descriptor` event **before** downstream prompt admission can block or throw, so a rejected admission still leaves a pre-turn log-only fact (L75).
- Edge cases / failure handling: A required durability checkpoint with no installed listener, or a failing listener, rejects the run with stable code `DURABILITY_FAILED` and the checkpoint failure as its cause; the manager records a failed Task whose detail says the latest state was not confirmed persisted and may be unavailable or stale on resume (L35). Before Agent publication, abort makes the provider reject only after its creation transaction has rolled back and reached quiescence; after publication the provider closes the creation-signal handoff race and attaches the same signal to the live run (L45). Before routing any by-id operation the manager **synchronously** compares its association with `ctx.agents.get(childId)`; a registry Agent with no association, or a different Agent, is an ownership conflict and the manager fails rather than adopting an idle Agent (L53). A failed initial Task or a process exit before the first child flush can leave an "unmaterialized child" — a child id with no durable header, reported unavailable by later by-id operations (L51).
- Surprising: Steering is admitted through a **message-owned receipt**: the default loop resolves it `admitted` only after `agent/step` and asynchronous prompt assembly succeed, the message is appended, immutable request history is captured, and `step/start` commits; terminal turn policy, cancellation and disposal resolve pending receipts `rejected` (L55). The note is explicit that admission is **not atomic or exactly-once** — two callers may still race a stopped child through paths outside the manager, and the Agent registry prevents duplicate same-session publication so the losing Task fails and its message is not delivered (L57, L121). A delivered message has no independent result: its effect is reflected in the current Task's eventual result, and the subagent layer adds no second completion injection (L69).
