### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-25-session-list-browsing-and-manual-order.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-07-25-session-list-browsing-and-manual-order.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the browsing and manual-order decision (same 61 lines, same five rejected alternatives).
- Key abstractions: Same `WorkspaceView.sessionIds` manual order; same `workspace.insertBefore({ workspaceId, sessionId, beforeSessionId? })` with DOM `insertBefore` semantics; same `workspace.rename({ workspaceId, title })`; same two-fact shell↔region contract `{ wide, expandSidebar }`.
- Data flow: Same two-mode browsing — WorkSpace renders peer rows in the manual order, In one list merges every session strictly newest-first by `updatedAt`; neither mode projects `parentId` into a list hierarchy, and the mode persists in the browser under `dsh.workspace.view` (L18).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "two ordering authorities cannot coexist" argument that killed activity pinning, and the DOM-`insertBefore` argument against a numeric index (an index drifts during the drag window, whereas an anchor is immune to prepends and filtered projections) (L12, L44). It also keeps the "trust the host completely" rule — the drop sends only the RPC, the client performs zero local reordering, and a failed move changes nothing (L46).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same typed error — the entity throws `WorkspaceMoveInvalidError` only for unaccounted session/anchor ids, the handler maps exactly that to `workspace-move-invalid`, and storage failures stay internal (L32). The client's upsert rejects snapshots older than the installed projection so a late unary response cannot roll back a newer frame (L34).
- Surprising: Faithfully keeps the "no optimistic reordering" reasoning — client-first reordering needs failure rollback and one more entangled state, and local/LAN round-trips are millisecond-scale, so waiting for the host response is imperceptible (L46). It also keeps the accepted scope reduction that flat mode supports neither reordering nor a create-in-workspace entry (L56).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-25-subagent-policy-inheritance.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-07-25-subagent-policy-inheritance.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the delegation-time policy capture decision (same 42 lines, same six rejected alternatives).
- Key abstractions: Same shared helpers `captureDelegatedPolicyOverrides` / `appendDelegatedPolicyOverrides` in `dsh-subagent`; same `permission/preset`, `sandbox/mode` and `approval/policy` event kinds; same `Session.firstLiveSeq` / `Session.inheritedEventCount` boundary.
- Data flow: Same capture point — the delegation boundary calls the helpers *before its first await*, so the one-shot driver and the continuable start both snapshot the parent's Auto/Full access identity, its explicit sandbox override, and pin the child's approval policy to `'never'` (L14).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the "unpublished setup" rule: the inherited facts become events during the child factory's unpublished setup, so the constructor's `firstLiveSeq` stays fixed and the fork lineage cut is unchanged, while the existing last-event-wins folds make the delegation snapshot beat stale fork history and a later child switch beat the snapshot (L16). It also keeps the "snapshot at delegation, not live resolution" argument — live resolution breaks the "two sessions never see each other's state" isolation invariant and lets a mid-run parent switch retroactively change a running child (L34).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same composition rule — a grandchild folds its parent's logged state, so the rule composes without a second inheritance mechanism (L16); the permission-preset and sandbox-policy services are optional, so compositions without them behave unchanged (L42). Ordinary session appends validate the inherited events before publication and persistence captures the complete unpublished log, so there is no second policy store or schema field (L20).
- Surprising: Faithfully keeps that Auto review does **not** turn the inherited preset event into an authorization receipt: every child call is classified again, medium-risk work needs an explicit authorisation naming action, target and scope, and high-risk work is always denied (L18). It also keeps the blocked-child experience — the ordinary denial marker, the deterministic rejection of escalation by the pinned `'never'` policy, and the `subagent:delegation` context statement telling the child to report the limitation instead of retrying (L24).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-25-workspace-ui-product-flow.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-07-25-workspace-ui-product-flow.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the workspace product flow (same 122 lines, same RPC table, same seven rejected alternatives).
- Key abstractions: Same six Host RPCs (`workspace.list` / `workspace.create` / `workspace.insertBefore` / `workspace.delete` / `session.create({ workspaceId, sessionId? })` / `session.create({ cwd })`); same `SessionManager` / `WorkspaceManager` / `SessionRuntime` / `WorkspaceRuntime` object layer; same five standard slot actions (`startSession`, `updateSessionPrompt`, `sendSession`, `open`, `toggleSidebar`).
- Data flow: Same zero-state rule — with no Workspace the page creates a frontend Workspace object and a frontend Session that targets it, neither writes to the Host, and the first send materialises the Workspace, attaches the Session and sends in that order (L52, L60). The same membership projection applies: an id must be in `Workspace.sessionIds` *and* its canonicalised `SessionHeader.cwd` must equal the Workspace path (L31).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the bootstrap discipline — when the durable marker is absent the Registry calls only `SessionPersistence.list()` to read header metadata, never `load` or `inspect`, never reads history and never parses event bodies; the marker is written last and bootstrap is reentrant (L33). It also keeps the "no `workspaceId` on SessionHeader" argument, because the index and the header would be two persistent ownership fields requiring double writes (L31).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same five-step recovery ladder — a Workspace creation failure leaves the input and error on the Workspace Intent; a pre-publication Session failure returns the Intent to an editable state and retries with the *same* preallocated SessionId; `workspace-attach-failed` proves the Session was published; a prompt failure retries only the send; and a page switch during creation means the old Session does not send automatically even if it is later published (L62, L63, L64, L65, L66).
- Surprising: Faithfully keeps that losing an RPC response or receiving a Host frame before the completion all converge through the preallocated SessionId and object identity, and that the Manager prioritises preserving the original object identity over creating a temporary second row with the same id (L68). It also keeps that "immediately create a Host Session in the zero state" was rejected because a page with no input would enter the Host lifecycle and change refresh semantics (L94).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-26-code-mode-trajectory-waterfall-spans.zh.md

- Edge cases / failure handling: Same "no new wire data, no new stores" constraint — both views read `codeDispatches` through the standard snapshot hook, so replay renders identically to live by construction (L20).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-26-ptc-chat-subcall-rows.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-07-26-ptc-chat-subcall-rows.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the PTC-mode chat sub-call decision (same 33 lines, same three rejected alternatives).
- Key abstractions: Same `ToolCallTree` fold of `tool/code-dispatch-start` / `tool/code-dispatch` into a private per-parent index; same recursive `ToolCallBlock.subCalls`; same `'conversation.chat.tool'` seat and `'tool.call.toolview'` keyed slot with `entryKey = Tool name`.
- Data flow: Same projection rule — sub-calls never join `nodes`, so the surface flow remains exactly the model-visible turn structure; live session projection and `projectConversationHistory` share the fold, and copy-on-write parent arrays plus path-copy projection keep unrelated roots and siblings reference-stable (L18). The render layer dispatches every atomic call through the same keyed slot, so a keyed registration takes over descendant and top-level calls without registration changes (L19).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the three rejections — folding sub-calls into `nodes` (it misrepresents the transcript, because the model made *one* call), hiding them until the parent row expands (the sub-calls are the story of a PTC turn), and a dedicated sub-call row component (a parallel component would drift; the nest wrapper is the only sub-call-specific chrome) (L25, L27, L29).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same details-panel rule — `materialFor` recursively searches `nodes` and `runningCalls`, so a selected descendant callId resolves to full args and complete output through the identical rendering path as a native settled call; the wire-consumer boundary also rejects cyclic parent relationships (L18, L21).
- Surprising: Faithfully keeps that custom toolview registrations apply to sub-calls *for free and deliberately* — there is no per-registration opt-out short of a component reading its own context, which no current consumer needs (L32).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-26-ptc-dispatch-log-spill.i18n.yaml
- Purpose: Bilingual-pair consistency record for the PTC-mode dispatch log spill note — the git blob hashes of its English and Chinese sides.
- Key abstractions: two `path: sha1` lines under the four-line pairing header naming the repair command with this note's path.
- Data flow: The translation-pairing verifier compares the recorded hashes against the working tree.
- Non-obvious design decisions: Both languages carry equal authority, so the Chinese side documents the same listener contract.
- Edge cases / failure handling: Hash drift is the only failure mode; the file is machine-written.
- Surprising: The note it pins introduces a *new* waterfall event (`tools/ptc-dispatch-log`) whose only purpose is to bound the durable copy of a sub-call result — and the pairing record keeps that "the log copy is not model context" premise stated identically in both languages.

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-26-ptc-dispatch-log-spill.zh.md
- Purpose: Chinese translation of `2026-07-26-ptc-dispatch-log-spill.md` — a line-for-line parallel rendering of the dispatch-log spill decision (same 32 lines, same three rejected alternatives).
- Key abstractions: Same scope-filtered `tools/ptc-dispatch-log` waterfall on the registry; same private `shapeDispatchLog` invoker handed to the bridge as a capability closure in `RunCodeBridgeOptions`; same `PtcDispatchLog` payload (outer execution, `agent` routing key, sub-call identity, default content).
- Data flow: Same boundary — the bridge runs the waterfall over each settled sub-dispatch *before* appending `tool/code-dispatch`; a listener may replace only the durable copy, which the model never sees, while the program still receives the structured `value` (L18). The listener runs as tracked work outside the program's result path, and when more than `maxParallelSubCalls` log tasks are pending the ordered commit loop waits, so a slow spill backend limits later sub-call starts instead of accumulating unlimited pending I/O (L18).
- Non-obvious design decisions: Preserves the three rejections — a plain byte limit inside the bridge without spill storage (truncation without a locator loses data replay or UIs may need), spilling inside the bridge by calling `ctx.spillStore` from `ptc.ts` (the registry would require the spill capability; the waterfall keeps the policy with the other spill decisions and lets compositions omit it), and reusing `tools/post-execute` for nested calls (post-execute can change the program-facing result, so nested calls deliberately skip it) (L24, L26, L28).
- Edge cases / failure handling: Same containment — if a listener throws, the invoker reports the thrown value safely and uses the original settled content; the run settlement still waits for every task inside the open turn (L18). The same `maxInlineBytes` limit, preview, locator, within-limit invariant and best-effort fallback are reused from the model-result listener (L19).
- Surprising: Faithfully keeps the one deliberate difference — the model-result listener skips `read` to prevent a `read → spill → read again` loop, while the dispatch-log listener *does* replace oversized `read` sub-call content because a log copy is not model context, so the loop cannot occur, and `read` is the tool most likely to produce a large log entry (L20).

### .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-26-ptc-dispatch-ui-foundation.i18n.yaml
- Purpose: Bilingual-pair consistency record for the PTC dispatch UI foundation note — the git blob hashes of its English and Chinese sides.
- Key abstractions: two `path: sha1` lines under the four-line header, whose repair command names `pnpm run verify-translation-pairing --write .agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-26-ptc-dispatch-ui-foundation.md`.
- Data flow: The translation-pairing verifier compares the recorded hashes against the working tree to detect a one-sided edit.
- Non-obvious design decisions: The header states that both languages carry equal authority, so the Chinese side is a maintained twin and the record is regenerated by tooling after every paired edit.
- Edge cases / failure handling: A mismatched hash pair is the only failure mode; the file is machine-written and carries no prose of its own.
- Surprising: The note it pins is the *foundation* of the PTC stack — it defines the full-content `tool/code-dispatch` event and the required `description` parameter that the later spill and sub-call-row notes build on — and its pairing record is the first entry in the trio that keeps that event's shape described identically in both languages.

## Ideas worth stealing

- **Compaction summarises by replaying the routed request's own prefix and appending the directive as a trailing user message, so the provider's warm KV cache is reused instead of thrown away** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-18-compaction-capability-seam.md:37` - The August harness compacts by summarising a range with a *fresh* request, which re-reads the whole prefix and pays full input cost every time. DSH instead reuses the exact provider/model that served the failed request and re-sends its prefix, so the KV cache stays warm and only the summarised span is new work. Directly transferable: the August `compactMessages` path could build its summariser request from the last routed request's prefix instead of the full history.

- **A session-stable opener is frozen per instance so the provider's prompt cache holds *by construction*, not by discipline** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-07-session-prefix.md:23` - "Reuse is structural, not disciplined: the cached product cannot change mid-session, so the provider's prompt cache holds by construction." The August harness has a per-session frozen memory index for exactly this reason, but DSH generalises it into a first-class seam (a request-only, header-logged prefix) with an explicit rule that a mid-session content change is *not* re-read until the next instance. Worth stealing as a rule for any August prompt section that must not churn: a change notice goes through the append-only history, not through a mutated prefix.

- **Canonicalise tool order *before* the assemble waterfall, so the request bytes — and therefore the cache prefix and the durable header — are deterministic** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-06-explicit-tool-order.md:23` - The default is lexicographic code-unit order (locale-independent, needs no configuration), and canonicalisation happens *before* the `system-prompt/assemble` waterfall so the deterministic list flows into the request header, the frozen request and the reconstruction checks. August's `toolDefinitions` ordering is the same class of hazard: a plugin-registration race changes request bytes, busts the provider cache and breaks request reconstruction. The one-line fix (sort before the waterfall) is cheap and the note also names the rejected alternative ("canonicalize in `llm.stream()`") and why it fails: it runs *after* the header is logged.

- **A misconfigured tool name fails the TURN, not the boot** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-06-explicit-tool-order.md:47` - "Every turn fails identically until the config is fixed; the process itself stays up (matching the repo rule that explicit config references must not be silently ignored — the enforcement point is the assembly because no earlier universal moment exists)." This is the exact shape August needs for its brain-config keys: reject at the first point where the referenced thing provably exists, keep the process alive, and make the failure *identical* on every attempt so it cannot be mistaken for a transient provider fault.

- **Provider and transport error text must never enter model context** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-24-provider-retry-policies.md:62` - "a transport or provider diagnostic is operational state, not conversation content. It can expose sensitive provider details and changes the retried request instead of repeating the failed request." Two transferable properties at once: the retried request must carry *the same derived context* as the failed one (so the retry is a true repeat), and the diagnostic must stay out of the prompt. August's stuck-request ladder and its `_stuckRequestEscape` reshaping are exactly this class, and this note gives the crisper statement of the invariant.

- **Retry policy is route-owned and captured at the final adapter boundary, so an in-flight request's recovery contract cannot be changed retroactively** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-24-provider-retry-policies.md:16` and `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-24-provider-retry-policies.md:44` - The policy is *registration state* on the provider route, not a retry-executor setting; `ctx.llm` binds the serving registration's immutable policy to the call at the last adapter boundary, so disposing or replacing the route mid-flight does not change the request's contract. Each scheduled retry appends a **non-surface** `llm/retry` event carrying the failed provider, the policy mode, a canonical resolved-policy key, the retry number and the delay — durable, observable, and invisible to the model (the UI renders an unbounded limit as `∞` rather than persisting `Infinity`). The canonical-key trick — retry history continues only for the same provider *and* the same resolved-policy key, so replacing a route with different limits starts a fresh count — is a cheap way to make a policy change observable.

- **Start-time capability mismatches are rejected LOUD, never accepted-then-ignored** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-21-subagent-capability-seam.md:49` - The service checks every requested start-time feature (`agentOptions`, `outputSchema`, `depthLimit`, `toolFilter`, `persona`) *before* delegating and raises `UNSUPPORTED_CAPABILITY` if the provider lacks it. This is the "a knob that always refuses is a lie" rule generalised: advertise only what the provider can honour, and refuse at the boundary rather than degrading silently. August's agent-permission model (where an empty permission list means "inherit", not "nothing") is the same family of confusion, and DSH's loud-rejection shape is the right answer.

- **Delegation snapshots the parent's policy at spawn time, and the snapshot is expressed as ordinary log events so existing folds order it correctly** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-25-subagent-policy-inheritance.md:16` and `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-25-subagent-policy-inheritance.md:34` - The inherited facts become `permission/preset` / `sandbox/mode` / `approval/policy` events in the child factory's *unpublished setup*, so the constructor's `firstLiveSeq` stays fixed and the fork lineage cut is unchanged, while the existing last-event-wins folds make the delegation snapshot beat stale fork history. The note names the alternative it rejected and why: "Live resolution walking `parentSession` at each call — it breaks the 'two sessions never see each other's state' isolation invariant … and makes a mid-run parent switch retroactively change a running child." Two August-relevant properties: a subagent cannot widen past its parent, and a mid-run parent tightening does not silently change a running child.

- **Loop hygiene is detected at exactly one seam and delivered as ADVICE, never as a block** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-08-repeat-tool-guard.md:20` and `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-08-repeat-tool-guard.md:25` - The guard counts on `tools/post-execute` — the one point where `(exec, result)` are seen together, so no cross-event pending map is needed, and it also fires for *denied* calls — and delivers reminders by prepending to the downstream decision's `additionalContexts`, never by replacing result content. The chain key is `(tool name, canonical arguments)` with canonicalisation being a deep key-sort plus `JSON.stringify`, so reordered argument keys are not new work. August's repeat/stagnation detection is the direct analogue, and the "post-execute also runs for denied calls" detail is exactly the kind of thing August's `_recordWorldDelta` credit must get right.

- **Instruction files are tracked by content digest, never by parsing rendered text, and there is deliberately no filesystem watcher** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-24-workspace-context.md:52` and `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-24-workspace-context.md:60` - "Every workspace context event stores versioned metadata with `{ action, scope, path, digest? }`, where `digest` is SHA-1 over the loaded content … The model-facing prompt has no HTML comments, hidden markers, or headings that are parsed back into state." And on detection: no watcher, only a "next successful structured touch, resumed-baseline reconciliation, or entering pre-step that restores a shadowed baseline"; "A provider failure produces no removal; absence is only accepted when all configured candidates in that scope were probed successfully." The "absence requires positive proof" rule is the transferable half — a read that failed is not evidence that a file was deleted.

- **A compatibility bridge's context injection is not a veto, and it must delegate before it enriches** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-06-30-hook-bridges.md:45` - "returning `enter` from a waterfall listener WITHOUT calling `next()` short-circuits every later `agent/pre-step` / `tools/post-execute` listener, so a policy/sandbox plugin registered after the bridge would never see the prompt." Each bridge therefore calls `next()` first and only a real `deny`/`block` short-circuits. This is a general waterfall-ordering bug class (August's gate-participation rule is the sibling), and the fix — delegate, then enrich — is a one-line discipline every hook/middleware author should be held to.

- **Model-facing search tools expose NO cursor; the harness follows cursors internally and caps the result count, and the spill mechanism owns output size** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-24-model-facing-session-query-tools.md:32` and `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-24-model-facing-session-query-tools.md:34` - "Neither search tool exposes a cursor, offset, page size, or model-controlled result limit … A capped result tells the model to narrow its query or filters; a generation change reports that the whole search must be retried." The rejected alternative states the reason plainly: a cursor is usually stale before the model can reuse it, because recording a tool result changes the generation. The spill half is equally transferable: the consumer neither imports `ctx.spillStore` nor implements a second truncation format — one generic `tools/post-execute` spill policy serves every tool. August's `retrieve_relevant_facts` / `brain_query` pagination is the exact place this transfers.

- **The durable log copy of a tool result is NOT model context, so it may be truncated even where the model-facing copy may not be** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-26-ptc-dispatch-log-spill.md:20` - "the model-result listener skips `read` to prevent a `read → spill → read again` loop. The dispatch-log listener also replaces oversized `read` sub-call content because a log copy is not model context, so that loop cannot occur, and `read` is the tool most likely to produce a large log entry." A small but genuinely transferable distinction: the same content can be governed by two different rules depending on whether it is headed for the model or for the transcript, and the reason (a re-read loop) is about the *destination*, not the content.

- **A progress phase is presentation state derived from live events, never a durable event** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-21-tui-verbose-status-line.md:15` - "`step/start` enters `waiting`; an `assistant/chunk` reasoning delta or reasoning block-start enters `thinking`; a text delta or text block-start enters `responding`; a `tool/call` enters `executing`. The event map is merge-extensible, so every other event kind falls through a default and leaves the phase unchanged." The reason it is not a session event is stated in the note: a durable, model-visible phase would demand a new session event for no model benefit. August's `update_state(phase=…)` is exactly this shape, and this note is the cleanest available statement of when a phase *should* become an event (it should not) and how to derive one that cannot desync.

- **Modes and enforcement knobs are orthogonal axes that never read or write each other** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-07-plan-mode.md:20` - "Modes are one axis and the enforcement knobs — the sandbox mode, the approval policy — are others: they never read or write each other, matching how Codex keeps its Plan/Default collaboration presets separate from its sandbox and approval settings." August's `set_agent_mode(chat|agent|code|orchestrator)` and its separate sandbox/approval knobs are the same design; this note is the citation for *why* they must stay independent, and for the "mode definition is deployment-configured guidance the model sees, while the mode IN FORCE is session state folded from its log" split.

- **A UI registry that plugins extend without a global bus event: store strings, coalesce the notification, and keep the observer list inside the service** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-24-configurable-tui-prompt-theme.md:16` - "Values are stored strings rather than callbacks … a registration, mutation, or disposal schedules one coalesced notification … That subscription is a direct in-service callback, not a Cordis event, so a value changing on its own schedule still repaints without a bus entry other consumers would never use." The rejected alternative is the interesting half: a global typed event was refused because it has exactly one consumer and would add a scoped-dispatch surface nobody observes. The containment detail is also worth stealing — a broken observer (synchronous throw, rejected promise, or an error hostile to string rendering) cannot starve the rest, and liveness is re-checked during delivery so a listener that unsubscribes another in the same burst silences it immediately.

- **Rendering must not fabricate a duration: unknown timing is drawn hollow and labelled, never as zero** - `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-26-code-mode-trajectory-waterfall-spans.md:19` - "Each lane carries a `timing` provenance tag: `measured` (pair observed), `running` (settle pending — extends to the window end at reduced opacity), or `unknown` (settle-only replay window, `callTime: null` — drawn hollow and titled 'duration unknown', never a fabricated 0 ms)." The three-valued provenance tag is a genuinely reusable pattern for any telemetry UI: distinguish *measured* from *inferred* from *unknown* in the data model, not just in the styling, so a consumer cannot mistake one for another.

## Files skipped (with reasons)

- None. Every one of the 260 files in the shard was opened and read. The shard contains no generated, vendored, binary or huge-data file: every entry is a UTF-8 markdown note or a small YAML pairing record, the largest being `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-06-sandbox.md` at 42,925 bytes (≈ 120 lines) and `.agents/notes/archived/feature/2026-07-26-ptc-dispatch-ui-foundation.zh.md` at 5,101 bytes, all comfortably read line by line. The 30 `.i18n.yaml` records are machine-*generated* (by `pnpm run verify-translation-pairing --write`) but are not generated *data* in the sense of the skip rule: each is six lines, was read in full, and each is given its own per-file note in the report and its own row in the ledger. The one generated file that *would* have qualified for a skip — `.agents/notes/archived/manifest.json`, 385,467 bytes, `generated-vendored` — is **not** in this shard; it is assigned to `dsh-notes-archived-rest#1` and was therefore neither read nor claimed here.

## Uncertainties

- **I did not touch the manifest, and I believe it is correct for this shard, but I did not verify it globally.** I read the 4th column of every row and kept the 260 rows whose owner-shard is exactly `dsh-notes-arch-feature#1`; every one of those 260 paths exists in the clone, and the 43 files this pass added are the only ones that were missing from the first pass's ledger. The manifest's `status` column is `pending` for *all* rows in the shard, so the manifest does not record which files a worker has covered — that is why the shard report and ledger are the real ledger here. I did not rewrite the shard column, per instruction.
- **The `.i18n.yaml` blob hashes are unverified against the working tree.** Each record states it holds "the git blob hash of each side as of the last confirmed-consistent state". I confirmed each file's *contents* (six lines: a four-line header plus the two `path: sha1` lines) but did not run `git hash-object` — the clone is untrusted and read-only for this pass and nothing in it may be executed. So the recorded hashes may legitimately have drifted since they were written; the note itself frames them as a last-confirmed snapshot, not a live invariant.
- **The notes are ARCHIVED and several are explicitly superseded; I could not establish which successor governs.** Examples read in this shard: the ACP notes (retired editor bridge), the session-prefix note (its seam was "later removed by the unified sourced-message decision"), the subagent-policy-inheritance note (its approval clause is superseded by the approvals-pinned decision) and the repeat-tool-guard note (it explicitly declines to escalate to a block). The superseding notes live in other directories (`../architecture/`, `../bug-fix/`, `../proposed/`, `../simplification/`) and belong to other shards, so I report each note's *own* claims and flag where it names its own successor. I did not open any of those sibling notes, so any statement of the form "the current mechanism is X" that would require them is out of scope here.
- **Cross-references inside the notes were not followed.** Each note links to package READMEs, `packages/*` sources and sibling notes (for example `packages/subagent/subagent/README.md`, `packages/core/agent/src/types.ts`, and the architecture/bug-fix notes). Those files belong to other shards of this research effort. I therefore describe the *mechanism as the note states it*, and did not independently confirm that the shipped code matches the note.
- **The first pass's ~1,290 line citations were re-checked only for range, not for meaning.** I ran a mechanical pass over every `(L##)` and `(L##–L##)` reference in this report: each one resolves to a real file (the file named in its own `###` heading) and to an in-range line — 0 out-of-range, 0 missing files. I did not re-derive the semantic claim behind each reference, and I deliberately did not add any line reference to a `.i18n.yaml` note, because those files are six lines long and a reference into the *paired* note would not resolve against the file the heading names.
- **Three defects in the first pass's output were repaired rather than reproduced:** a duplicate `### …/2026-06-14-acp-agent-client-protocol.md` heading whose body was the placeholder `- Purpose: test note`; a `### …/2026-07-20-windows-tui-support.i18n.yaml` heading that had been glued onto the end of the previous note's last line; and the missing `### …/2026-07-06-explicit-tool-order.i18n.yaml` section, which existed as a ledger row but had no note. The shard-shape section was also a `PLACEHOLDER-SHAPE` stub and has been written.
